hydrodynamic limit of non-gradient exclusion process Tadahisa Funaki, Chenlin Gu, Han Wang Tadahisa Funaki Beijing Institute of Mathematical Sciences and Applications, Beijing, China Email address: funaki@bimsa.cn Chenlin Gu Yau Mathematical Sciences Center, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China Email address: gclmath@tsinghua.edu.cn Han Wang Qiuzhen College, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China Email address: wanghan21@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn Abstract. For the non-gradient exclusion process, we prove the quantitative homogenization of the diffusion matrix and the conductivity by local functions. The proof relies on the renormalization approach developed by Armstrong, Kuusi, Mourrat, and Smart, while the new challenge here is the hard core constraint of particle number on every site. Therefore, a coarse-grained method is proposed to lift the configuration to a larger space without exclusion, and a gradient coupling between two systems is applied to capture the spatial cancellation. We then strengthen the convergence rate to be uniform concerning the density, and integrate it into the work by Funaki, Uchiyama, and Yau [ IMA Vol. Math. Appl. , 77 (1996), pp. 1-40.] to yield a quantitative hydrodynamic limit. Our new approach avoids showing the characterization of closed forms and provides stronger results. The extension is discussed for the model in the presence of disorder on the bonds. MSC 2010: 82C22, 35B27, 60K35. Keywords: interacting particle systems, non-gradient exclusion process, Kawasaki dynamics, diffusion matrix, quantitative homogenization, hydrodynamic limit. Contents 1 Introduction 2 Analytic tools 3 Coarse-grained lifting 4 Subadditive quantities 5 Renormalization under grand canonical ensemble 6 Density-free local corrector with uniform convergence 7 Quantitative hydrodynamic limit 8 Extension to disordered lattice gas A Sub-additivity and Whitney inequality B Proof of L^{\infty} norm using mixing time C Proofs of lemmas and proposition in Section References 1. Introduction The diffusion matrix plays an important role in the study of the large-scale behaviors of interacting particle systems. Among these systems, some are classified as the gradient models if the current of the conserved quantity can be written as a sum of the difference between a local function and its spatial shift, and the others are called the non-gradient models . Unlike the gradient models, the non-gradient models usually require more techniques to derive the hydrodynamic limit, because the scaling yields a diverging factor and, moreover, the diffusion matrix does not have an explicit expression and one needs to add a correction. This idea goes back to the seminal work [ 78 ] of Varadhan, who studied the Ginzburg–Landau model. Later, the hydrodynamic limit was proved in several classical particle systems of non-gradient type: the generalized symmetric exclusion process (GSEP) by Kipnis, Landim and Olla [ 55 ] ; the lattice gas , also known as the non-gradient exclusion process or speed-change Kawasaki dynamics , by Funaki, Uchiyama and Yau [ 36 ] , the general lattice gas with mixing condition by Varadhan and Yau [ 79 ] ; the multi-type simple symmetric exclusion process (multi-type SSEP) by Quastel [ 73 ] , etc. The equilibrium fluctuation in non-gradient models was studied later in [ 33 , 61 , 20 ] , and the regularity of the diffusion matrix was discussed in a series of work [ 58 , 69 , 70 , 71 , 16 ] . One can also refer to [ 76 , 54 ] for the basic background and [ 75 ] for the relation between the gradient condition and the Green–Kubo formula. It is natural to ask the convergence rate, and the quantitative hydrodynamic limit has received attentions recently. The sharp estimate of the relative entropy was obtained in [ 52 ] by Jara and Menezes for a weakly asymmetric exclusion, which is an important step to derive the non-equilibrium fluctuation. Based on [ 47 ] , the quantitative results were developed by Dizdar, Menz, Otto, and Wu for the Ginzburg–Landau model in [ 28 , 27 ] . Very recently, Menegaki and Mouhot proposed a consistence-stability approach in [ 64 ] to obtain the quantitative hydrodynamic limit in 1-Wasserstein distance for several models including the zero-range process, the Ginzburg–Landau model, and the simple exclusion process (see [ 63 , Chapter 5.5] ). However, all these results are for the gradient model, and there are no quantitative results for the non-gradient model in the literature to the best of our knowledge. This is because, as mentioned in the last paragraph, a diverging factor appears and the diffusion matrix is more complicated in the non-gradient model. Such obstacle was already observed in the proof in [ 36 , 33 ] , where several key error terms are finally reduced to the approximation of the diffusion matrix or the conductivity ; see [ 36 , (2.5) and Section 5] . These two fundamental quantities are defined using variational formulas and are related by the Einstein relation. Therefore, both of them can be approximated qualitatively by a sequence of local functions, but the convergence rate is unknown. In this paper, we answer the question above using a concrete construction of the desired local functions. As Varadhan observed the link between the interacting systems and homogenization, in the sense of averaging and gradient replacement that kills the diverging factor, in the earlier work [ 78 ] , the new improvement comes from the recent progress in quantitative homogenization theory; see [ 15 , 14 , 9 , 10 , 6 , 5 , 11 , 7 ] based on the renormalization approach, and [ 68 , 66 , 42 , 43 , 40 , 44 , 41 ] based on another approach using spectral inequalities. As an example, for the \nabla\phi interface model studied in [ 35 ] , a quantitative hydrodynamic limit is obtained in [ 4 ] using the renormalization approach. Also inspired by the renormalization approach, [ 38 ] studies a similar diffusion matrix problem in continuous configuration space, and a quantitative equilibrium fluctuation is obtained recently in [ 49 ] under the same setting. These works pave the way for the quantitative homogenization theory in interacting particle systems, but the continuous configuration model there relaxes the hard core constraint by allowing arbitrarily large number of particles in the unit volume. As a consequence, to apply the existing results to a lattice particle model of exclusion rule still meets technical challenges in math. The present paper aims to resolve these difficulties and is the first example to establish quantitative homogenization theory on the non-gradient exclusion process. Our main result not only generalizes [ 38 ] to the non-gradient exclusion process, but also improves in the sense that we construct one local corrector to realize uniform convergence of conductivity for every particle density. Moreover, this density-uniform homogenization can be integrated into the relative entropy method in the classical work [ 36 ] , and then establishes a quantitative hydrodynamic limit; namely, our result provides the convergence rate in the hydrodynamic limit for non-gradient exclusion process. We emphasize that our method is new and, in particular, avoids to prove the characterization of closed forms. The latter is usually required and useful to show the hydrodynamic limit for non-gradient models in qualitative level, but the convergence rate was not accessible. We believe that our proof is robust, as it extends to the cases where the external disorder is posed on the bonds. We hope to cover other models in future work, including GSEP, the multi-type SSEP, and the exclusion process reversible under the general Gibbs measure. 1.1. Main results In this part, we state our main results. We recall quickly the necessary notations of the exclusion process and the results in the previous work. Let {\mathbb{Z}^{d}} be the Euclidean lattice and we use \mathcal{X}:=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} to stand for the configuration of particles under exclusion rule. The element of \mathcal{X} will be denoted by \eta=\{\eta_{x}:x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\} . Here \eta_{x}=0 means the site x is vacant and \eta_{x}=1 means the site is occupied by one particle. We denote by y\sim x for x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} if |x-y|=1 . Then \{x,y\} is called an (undirected) bond. For every \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , we denote by \Lambda^{*} the set of bonds in \Lambda that (1.1) \displaystyle\Lambda^{*}:=\{\{x,y\}:x,y\in\Lambda,x\sim y\}. For x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , the exchange operator \eta^{x,y} is defined as \displaystyle(\eta^{x,y})_{z}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\eta_{z},&\qquad z\neq x,y;\\ \eta_{y},&\qquad z=x;\\ \eta_{x},&\qquad z=y.\end{array}\right. Especially, when b=\{x,y\} is a bond, we also write \eta^{b} instead of \eta^{x,y} , and define the Kawasaki operator \pi_{b}\equiv\pi_{x,y} (1.5) \displaystyle\pi_{b}f(\eta):=f(\eta^{b})-f(\eta). For every x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , the translation operator \tau_{x} is defined as (1.6) \displaystyle(\tau_{x}\eta)_{y}:=\eta_{x+y}, and for function f on \mathcal{X} , we also define \tau_{x}f as (1.7) \displaystyle(\tau_{x}f)(\eta)=f(\tau_{x}\eta). We also consider a family of local functions (1.8) \displaystyle\{c_{b}(\eta)\equiv c_{x,y}(\eta)=c_{y,x}(\eta);b=\{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}\}, which determine the jump rate of particles on the nearest bonds. We suppose the following conditions for the jump rate throughout the paper without specific explanation. Hypothesis 1.1 . The following conditions are supposed for \{c_{b}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}} . (1) Non-degenerate and local: c_{x,y}(\eta) depends only on \{\eta_{z}:|z-x|\leqslant\mathbf{r}\} for some integer \mathbf{r}>0 , and is bounded on two sides 1\leqslant c_{x,y}(\eta)\leqslant\lambda . (2) Spatially homogeneous: for all \{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*} , c_{x,y}=\tau_{x}c_{0,y-x} . (3) Detailed balance under Bernoulli measures: c_{x,y}(\eta) is independent of \{\eta_{x},\eta_{y}\} . The non-gradient exclusion process on {\mathbb{Z}^{d}} is defined by the generator below (1.9) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}:=\sum_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}c_{b}(\eta)\pi_{b}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}:|x-y|=1}c_{x,y}(\eta)\pi_{x,y}. This model is also called the speed-change Kawasaki dynamics or the lattice gas in the literature, and we will also use these names alternatively from time to time in the paper. This model is known of non-gradient type, i.e. we cannot find any function \{h_{i,j}\}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d} such that c_{0,e_{i}}(\eta)(\eta_{e_{i}}-\eta_{0})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left((\tau_{e_{j}}h_{i,j})(\eta)-h_{i,j}(\eta)\right) for general \{c_{b}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}} , with \{e_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d} the canonical basis of {\mathbb{Z}^{d}} . The hydrodynamic limit of the speed-change Kawasaki dynamics on torus is proved in [ 36 ] . Let \mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}:=(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})^{d} be the lattice torus of scale N , where we can define all the notations by replacing {\mathbb{Z}^{d}} with \mathbb{T}^{d}_{N} . We denote by \mathcal{X}_{N}:=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}} the configuration space on \mathbb{T}^{d}_{N} , and define \eta^{N}(t)=\{\eta^{N}_{x}(t),x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}\} as the \mathcal{X}_{N} -valued Markov jump process on torus governed by the generator \mathcal{L}_{N}:=N^{2}\mathcal{L} , the counterpart of ( 1.9 ) on \mathbb{T}^{d}_{N} . The macroscopic empirical measure of \eta^{N}(t) is defined as the following measure on \mathbb{T}^{d} (1.10) \displaystyle\rho^{N}(t,{\mathrm{d}}v):=N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}}\eta^{N}_{x}(t)\delta_{x/N}({\mathrm{d}}v), and the limit is the unique (classical) solution of nonlinear diffusion equation (1.11) \displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho(t,v)=\nabla\cdot(D(\rho(t,v))\nabla\rho(t,v)),\qquad(t,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}, provided that the initial profile \rho^{N}(0,{\mathrm{d}}v) has a smooth limit \rho(0,v) . Here \mathbb{T}^{d}={\mathbb{R}^{d}}/{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} is the continuous torus and D:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} is the diffusion matrix defined by the Einstein relation (1.12) \displaystyle D(\rho):=\frac{\mathbf{c}(\rho)}{2\chi(\rho)}, where \chi(\rho) is the compressibility (1.13) \displaystyle\chi(\rho):=\rho(1-\rho), and \mathbf{c}(\rho) is the effective conductivity defined as follows. Let \mathcal{F}_{0} stand for the local function space on \mathcal{X} and \mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}:=(\mathcal{F}_{0})^{d} , and \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho} stand for the expectation under Bernoulli product measure of density \rho\in[0,1] . We construct at first a quadratic form with respect to the {\mathbb{R}^{d}} -valued function F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} (1.14) \displaystyle\forall\xi\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}},\quad\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)\xi=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|x|=1}\left\langle{c_{0,x}\left(\xi\cdot\left\{x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})-\pi_{0,x}(\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{y}F)\right\}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Then \mathbf{c}(\rho) is the minimization of \mathbf{c}(\rho;F) (1.15) \displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho)\xi:=\inf_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}}\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)\xi. See [ 76 , Part II, Proposition 2.2] for the relation between the diffusion matrix D(\rho) and the Green–Kubo formula. It is proved in [ 16 ] that D(\rho) is continuously extendable to \rho=0,1 and (1.16) D\in C^{\infty}([0,1]). Furthermore, the diffusion matrix D(\rho) is uniformly positive and bounded: (1.17) \displaystyle\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }\rho\in[0,1],\qquad c_{*}|\xi|^{2}\leqslant\xi\cdot D(\rho)\xi\leqslant c^{*}|\xi|^{2}, where 0<c_{*}<c^{*}<\infty ; see [ 34 , Lemma 12.1] . The hydrodynamic limit in [ 36 , Theorem 1.1] states the following convergence: let P stand for the probability space of the process (\eta^{N}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}} and \rho(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)=\rho(t,v){\mathrm{d}}v . For every \phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}) and \varepsilon>0 , we have (1.18) \displaystyle P\left[\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho^{N}(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)\right|>\varepsilon\right]\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}0, assuming that the distribution of \eta^{N}(0) is close to the local equilibrium state with a density profile \rho_{0}(v) , which satisfies following Hypothesis 1.2 . Hypothesis 1.2 . The initial value \rho_{0}=\rho_{0}(v) of the nonlinear diffusion equation ( 1.11 ) is smooth (i.e., \rho_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}) ) and satisfies 0<\rho_{0}(v)<1 for every v\in\mathbb{T}^{d} . Hypothesis 1.2 , together with the properties ( 1.16 ) and ( 1.17 ) of D(\rho) , ensures the unique smooth classical solution of the equation ( 1.11 ) in the class {\rho(t,v)\in C^{1,3}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})} ; see [ 57 , Theorem 6.1, p.452] , and the discussion in [ 34 , Section 10] . Moreover, by the maximum principle (see [ 29 , p.389] ), we have 0<\rho(t,v)<1 . The proof of the hydrodynamic limit in [ 36 ] relies on the relative entropy method . Roughly, the relative entropy h_{N}(t) measures how close the empirical measure \eta^{N}(t) is to the solution \rho(t,\cdot) ; see Section 7.1 for the definition of h_{N}(t) . The assumption in ( 1.18 ) about the initial data is h_{N}(0)=o(1) as N\to\infty , and ( 1.18 ) was proved via h_{N}(t)\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}0 . More precisely, one key step ( [ 36 , Theorem 2.1, Corollary 2.1] ) proved that, for every \beta>0 and small \delta>0 , the following estimate for h_{N}(t) is valid (1.19) \displaystyle h_{N}(t)\leqslant h_{N}(0)+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}h_{N}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s+C(\beta+1)\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F_{N})|+\frac{C}{\beta}+Q_{N}. One can find the details of the error term Q_{N}\,(=Q^{\Omega_{1}}_{N}+Q^{LD}_{N}+Q^{En}_{N}+Q^{\Omega_{2}}_{N}) in Proposition 7.15 . The function F_{N} in ( 1.19 ) aims to better approximate the reference measure in h_{N}(t) (see ( 7.5 ) for details), and the quantity R(\rho;F) in ( 1.19 ) comes from the conductivity ( 1.14 ) and ( 1.15 ), which is defined as (1.20) \displaystyle R(\rho;F):=\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)-\mathbf{c}(\rho). To conclude the decay of relative entropy h_{N}(t) , we expect naturally that the error term Q_{N} vanishes as N\to\infty . We need to take a large \beta such that C/\beta on the right-hand side of ( 1.19 ) is small, and then apply Gronwall’s inequality and the entropy inequality. Therefore, the term R(\rho;F_{N}) in ( 1.19 ) also contributes the decay, and it was proved in [ 36 , Lemma 2.1] that (1.21) \displaystyle\inf_{F\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F)|=0, where |R|:=\big(\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}R_{ij}^{2}\big)^{1/2} stands for the norm of the matrix R=(R_{ij})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d} . The object of this paper is to give a convergence rate for \rho^{N}(t,\cdot)\to\rho(t,\cdot) as N\to\infty , improving the qualitative convergence in probability stated in ( 1.18 ). As explained briefly above, one essential step is the decay of R(\rho;F_{N}) , and we need to study ( 1.21 ) more precisely. Actually, the refinement of ( 1.21 ) can be considered as a quantitative homogenization of the fundamental quantity \mathbf{c}(\rho) , which is our main result stated as follows. In the statement, \Lambda_{L}:=(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{d}\cap{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} stands for a hypercube of side length around L\in\mathbb{R}_{+} , and \mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}(\Lambda_{L}) is the subset of \mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} which contains \sigma(\{\eta_{x}\}_{x\in\Lambda_{L}}) -measurable local functions. Theorem 1.3 . Under Hypothesis 1.1 , there exists an exponent \gamma(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})>0 and a positive constant C(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})<\infty , such that (1.22) \displaystyle\inf_{F_{L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F_{L})|\leqslant CL^{-\gamma}. We will also give the concrete construction of the local function achieving the estimate above in our proof; see Section 6.4 for details. Then as expected, we can insert the estimate ( 1.22 ) in ( 1.19 ), and obtain a quantitative hydrodynamic limit after careful investigation. In the statement, H^{-\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d}) is the standard Sobolev space on \mathbb{T}^{d} with negative index -\alpha . We refer to Section 7.1 for the details of relative entropy and other related settings. Theorem 1.4 . Under Hypothesis 1.1 , and we assume Hypothesis 1.2 for the initial value \rho_{0} with the initial entropy condition h_{N}(0)\leqslant C_{0}N^{-\kappa_{0}} for some constants {C_{0},\kappa_{0}>0} . Then, for every \alpha>d/2 and T>0 , there exist \kappa(\kappa_{0},d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})>0 and {C(C_{0},\alpha,T,\rho_{0},\kappa_{0},d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})>0} such that the following estimate holds (1.23) \displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\|\rho^{N}(t,\cdot)-\rho(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\Big]\leqslant CN^{-\kappa}. The quantitative hydrodynamic limit was shown for several kinds of gradient models; see [ 28 , 64 , 52 ] . Our Theorem 1.4 , which is shown for non-gradient model, is formulated similar to [ 28 ] , in which one-dimensional Ginzburg–Landau lattice model of gradient type was studied and, in their case, d=1 , \alpha=1 and \kappa=2/3 . In our case, the range of \alpha is (d/2,\infty) , which is taken as widely as possible for the measure \rho^{N}(t) on \mathbb{T}^{d} . Our \kappa>0 is determined from several factors and small. The optimal exponent suggested by the central limit theorem should be \kappa\simeq d . 1.2. Strategy of the proof The outline of this paper is presented in Figure 1 . It can be divided into 4 blocks. The main body is the orange block in the center, which consists of Sections 4 , 5 , 8 , and contains a proof of quantitative homogenization. The argument roughly follows [ 11 , Chapter 2] , which was developed by Armstrong, Kuusi, Mourrat, and Smart, and has been applied to many other models. However, several analytic inputs are required to apply this method to the non-gradient exclusion setting. They are resumed in the red block on the left, consisting of Section 3 and Proposition 2.6 . They are also the main technical progresses in this paper. Especially, we need to handle the constraint of particle numbers. The blue block on the right corresponds to Section 6 , where we improve the homogenization result, so it becomes independent of density as stated in Theorem 1.3 . Finally, the green block at the bottom refers to the quantitative hydrodynamic limit in Section 7 . We further explain the 4 blocks one by one in the following paragraphs. Section 3 Coarse-grained lifting Proposition 3.9 Weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality Proposition 2.6 Modified Caccioppoli inequality Section 5 Quantitative homogenization via renormalization ( Extension in Section 8 ) Section 4 Subadditive quantities Section 6.3 Construction of density-free corrector Section 7 Quantitative hydrodynamic limit Relative entropy method [ 36 ] Section 6.1 Regularity of local corrector Section 6.2 Convergence under canonical ensemble Lemma 6.5 Local equivalence of ensembles Figure 1. The outline of proof. 1.2.1. Homogenization via the renormalization approach This part concerns the orange block in Figure 1 . The quantitative homogenization via the renormalization approach was developed in [ 15 , 14 , 9 , 10 , 6 , 5 ] ; see monographs [ 11 , 7 ] and [ 67 ] for a gentle introduction. This renormalization approach has shown its robustness in a number of other settings including the parabolic equations [ 1 ] , the finite-difference equations on percolation clusters [ 3 , 22 , 24 ] , the differential forms [ 23 ] , the “ \nabla\phi ” interface model [ 21 , 12 , 13 , 4 ] , and the Villain model [ 25 ] . Recently, [ 38 , 39 , 49 ] also generalize the theory to an interacting particle system in continuous space without exclusion. The heart of the renormalization approach requires a finite-volume approximation with sub-additivity. Therefore, let us first reformulate ( 1.14 ) and ( 1.15 ). Consider a formal sum \ell_{\xi}=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}(\xi\cdot x)\eta_{x} , then we notice that the term \xi\cdot x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0}) in ( 1.14 ) is \displaystyle\xi\cdot x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})=-\pi_{0,x}\ell_{\xi}. Moreover, we denote by the formal sum \Gamma_{F}:=\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{y}F given F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} . Thus, ( 1.14 ) is the Dirichlet energy of a linear statistic plus some correction term \Gamma_{F} (1.24) \begin{split}\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)\xi&=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}(\ell_{\xi}+\xi\cdot\Gamma_{F}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &=\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{c_{x,x+e_{i}}\left(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}(\ell_{\xi}+\xi\cdot\Gamma_{F}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} Here \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and the passage to the second line is based on stationarity. We then propose the following expression as a natural finite-volume approximation of ( 1.15 ) (1.25) \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)\xi:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{\xi}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Here \Lambda^{-} is the interior of \Lambda and \overline{\Lambda^{*}} is the set of bonds issued from \Lambda (see ( 1.40 ), ( 1.41 ) for details). \mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) is the set of \sigma(\{\eta_{x}\}_{x\in\Lambda^{-}}) -measurable local functions, and \mathcal{L}_{\Lambda} is the generator on \Lambda (1.26) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v:=\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}c_{b}\pi_{b}v. A well-known integration by part formula under \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho} also tells us (1.27) \displaystyle\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}, so ( 1.25 ) can be written as \displaystyle\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)\xi \displaystyle=\inf_{v\in\ell_{\xi}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle\approx\inf_{v\in\ell_{\xi}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{c_{x,x+e_{i}}(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. We throw away the boundary layer in the second line, then this formula is very close to the version in ( 1.24 ). We expect that \overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda) converges to \mathbf{c}(\rho) when \Lambda\nearrow{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} . A similar definition like ( 1.25 ) can also be posed under the canonical ensemble (1.28) \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda,N)\xi:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{\xi}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v)}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}, where \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N} is the expectation under the uniform measure of N particles in \Lambda . Notice that the quantity \hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda,N) still depends on the configuration outside \Lambda . On the other hand, because the jump rate c is of finite range \mathbf{r} by Hypothesis 1.1 , the influence from the boundary layer vanishes when \Lambda\nearrow{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} . Thus \hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda,N) should be close to \mathbf{c}(N/|\Lambda|) in large scale. The convergence of these two quantities are intermediate steps to prove Theorem 1.3 , and are also related to the CLT variance estimate in [ 36 , Section 5] . Theorem 1.5 . Under Hypothesis 1.1 , there exists a constant C(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})<\infty and two exponents {\gamma_{1}(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r}),\gamma_{2}(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})>0} such that for every L,M\in\mathbb{N}_{+} , (1.29) \displaystyle\left|\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda_{L})-\mathbf{c}(\rho)\right|\leqslant CL^{-\gamma_{1}}, and (1.30) \displaystyle\left|\hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda_{L},M)-\mathbf{c}(M/|\Lambda_{L}|)\right|\leqslant CL^{-\gamma_{2}}. The convergence of quantities in Theorem 1.5 is largely studied using the renormalization approach, because \overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda) defined in ( 1.25 ) satisfies the sub-additivity. That is, for disjoint union \Lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}\Lambda^{(i)} , we have (1.31) \displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|\Lambda^{(i)}|}{|\Lambda|}\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda^{(i)}). To prove it, it suffices to “glue” the minimizers in \{\Lambda^{(i)}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N} together, which yields a sub-minimizer for the functional of \overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda) . Then, \overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda) admits a limit, saying \overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho) , when \Lambda\nearrow{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} . The coincidence \overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)=\mathbf{c}(\rho) is not contained directly from their definitions, but can be justified later in Proposition 6.9 . The sub-additivity ( 1.31 ) only implies a qualitative convergence. To quantify the rate, an important observation from [ 11 , Chapter 2] is another dual quantity \overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda) . It satisfies \overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)\leqslant\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda) , so we can use its gap \left(\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)\right) to measure the convergence rate. Surprisingly, the gap itself \left(\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)\right) roughly satisfies the sub-additivity like ( 1.31 ) (1.32) \displaystyle\left(\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)\right)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|\Lambda^{(i)}|}{|\Lambda|}\left(\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda^{(i)})-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda^{(i)})\right). See ( 4.24 ) for its rigorous version. This non-trivial property hints the quantitative homogenization. The complete proof is presented in Section 4 and 5 . Remark 1.6 . We have three remarks about Theorem 1.5 . (1) Recalling the Einstein relation ( 1.12 ), the results in Theorem 1.5 also imply the convergence rate of the diffusion matrix D(\rho) , for all \rho\in(0,1) , by local functions or the finite-volume approximation. (2) The choice of the notation \overline{{\Lambda}^{*}} here is just for technical convenience. Lemma A.1 ensures the stability of Theorem 1.5 in the general domain, so the statement still holds if one replaces \overline{{\Lambda}^{*}} in ( 1.26 ) by the canonical notation \Lambda^{*} defined in ( 1.1 ). (3) We also obtain an estimate similar to ( 1.29 ) when the disorder is posed on bonds. To simplify the notation, we leave the related discussion in Section 8 . 1.2.2. Two analytic inequalities and coarse-grained lifting This part explains the red block in Figure 1 . Besides the observation of sub-additivity ( 1.32 ), the proof of Theorem 1.5 also needs two analytic inequalities: (1) the Caccioppoli inequality; (2) the multiscale Poincaré inequality. The two key inequalities are well-developed for the elliptic equation on {\mathbb{R}^{d}} , but can become challenging in other settings. We should also highlight that, the two inequalities are more than the technical estimates, but the essentials of quantitative homogenization, because they characterize the elliptic conditions in the large scale; see the recent work for the homogenization in high contrast [ 2 ] , [ 8 ] . In the exclusion process, these inequalities are not accessible directly. The main contribution in this paper is to relax them respectively as the modified Caccioppoli inequality and the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality , which are explained in the following paragraphs in detail. The classical Caccioppoli inequality describes the inner regularity of the elliptic equations, but seems missing in the particle systems due to the influence of particles near the boundary. Therefore, the modified Caccioppoli inequality is developed in the previous work [ 38 , Proposition 3.9] , which differs from the classical one, but captures the same spirit. In the present work, its counterpart in the exclusion process is also recovered in Proposition 2.6 . The proof requires more work due to the microscopic behavior; see Lemma 2.1 and Lemma 2.7 . As new inputs, the Glauber derivative and the reverse Efron–Stein inequality are also involved. The multiscale Poincaré inequality (see [ 11 , Proposition 1.12 and Corollary 1.14] for example) improves the estimate of the classical Poincaré inequality when the function has the spatial cancellation property, which is the case in homogenization. In particle systems, this inequality was derived in the previous work [ 38 ] , but meets obstacles in the present paper due to the exclusion rule. Precisely, we need the H^{2} -estimate in the proof (see [ 11 , Corollary 1.14] ), which is only true for the standard Laplacian operator \Delta rather than a general variable-speed divergence form -\nabla\cdot\mathbf{a}(x)\nabla in PDE setting. In exclusion processes, one may expect the generator of SSEP to be a natural analogue of \Delta . Unfortunately, it is not totally correct; see Remark 2.11 for details. A heuristic explanation is that, particles in SSEP are still less free than independent random walks. For this reason, we believe the multiscale Poincaré inequality should live in the independent particles \widetilde{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{N}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} where \mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,3,\cdots\} , and we prove it in Section 2.2 . Then a crucial problem is how to apply an inequality in \widetilde{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{N}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} to the functions in \mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} . A similar problem was also posed in the percolation setting, and a possible solution is the coarse-grained strategy; see [ 3 , 22 , 48 , 24 ] . We hope to implement this idea in the exclusion processes: for every function u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} , we aim to find a coarsened function [u]:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R} in the larger space such that for every \eta\in\mathcal{X} as the grain of \widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}} , it satisfies (1.33) \displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}\simeq\eta\Longrightarrow[u](\widetilde{\eta})\simeq u(\eta). A naive candidate for grain \eta is the one close to \widetilde{\eta} under some distance. However, different from the Bernoulli percolation setting, the space of grain \mathcal{X} is a very sparse subset of \widetilde{\mathcal{X}} in particle systems, so we face the curse of dimension which may extremely enlarge the error in ( 1.33 ). This is also the key difficulty compared to the previous work [ 38 ] . Our solution turns out to be not only a coarse-grained method of functions, but also a lift from \mathcal{X} to \widetilde{\mathcal{X}} , i.e. we can represent the function on Kawasaki dynamics using a coupled system of independent particles. More precisely, for every \widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}} , we set its grain [\widetilde{\eta}]\in\mathcal{X} as \displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\qquad[\widetilde{\eta}]_{x}:=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\geqslant 1\right\}}, and define the coarsened function of u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} as \displaystyle[u]:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad[u](\widetilde{\eta}):=u([\widetilde{\eta}]). This coarse-grained lifting is introduced in Section 3 . Based on this technique, we also obtain a gradient coupling between two systems (see Propositions 3.2 and 3.6 ), and a weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality on Kawasaki dynamics. They are the main tools to evaluate the flatness of the functions in Proposition 5.3 . Figure 2. An illustration for the coarse-grained lifting between the Kawasaki dynamics and independent particles. 1.2.3. Regularity and uniform convergence This part further develops the idea in the blue block in Figure 1 . Usually the convergence rate depends on the particle density, so let us explain why a uniform convergence is valid. A first qualitative argument is that, our finite-volume approximation decreases to the limit, and the limit function \rho\mapsto\mathbf{c}(\rho) is continuous thanks to [ 69 ] , so Dini’s theorem applies and the convergence is locally uniform. At the quantitative level, we should track the dependence of density carefully in analysis. Especially, the two key inputs above are both robust with respect to the density. We also notice that \mathbf{c} has a trivial bound by \chi(\rho)=\rho(1-\rho) , and this can help us at two endpoints. We still need some more ingredients to pass the results from Theorem 1.5 to Theorem 1.3 . The uniform estimates in ( 1.29 ) can be seen as a weak version \displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\inf_{F_{\rho,L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}R(\rho;F_{\rho,L})\leqslant CL^{-\gamma_{1}}, while quantity in ( 1.22 ) is a strong version, and usually we have \displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\inf_{F_{\rho,L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}R(\rho;F_{\rho,L})\leqslant\inf_{F_{L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}R(\rho;F_{L}). We do not know whether there exists any duality property in the function R(\rho;F) , thus we make the proof by a direct construction. The minimizer F in this variational problem is actually the correctors in homogenization theory. In the renormalization step, we already get a candidate \phi_{\rho,\Lambda,\xi} for the problem ( 1.25 ), but it has dependence on the density. Our main task is to remove this dependence, so we propose a modified version of the local corrector: for disjoint union \Lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}\Lambda^{(i)} , we make a combination of the local correctors on every subset (1.34) \displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}_{\hat{\rho},\Lambda,\xi}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{\hat{\rho},\Lambda_{i},\xi}, where \hat{\rho} is an empirical density on the domain \Lambda instead of a fixed designed density. Insert this function in the problem ( 1.25 ), we obtain a uniform convergence under the grand canonical ensemble by the following reasons. (1) The homogenization appears in the large scale, so ( 1.31 ) is nearly an equality and \sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{\rho,\Lambda_{i},\xi} nearly equals \phi_{\rho,\Lambda,\xi} . (2) Given an empirical density \hat{\rho} , each local corrector lives as if under the grand canonical ensemble thanks to the local equivalence of ensembles. This is also the trick in the proof of ( 1.30 ). (3) The empirical density \hat{\rho} may also fluctuate when applying the Kawasaki operator, but this can be handled. On the one hand, we have the regularity of the mapping \rho\mapsto\phi_{\rho,\Lambda_{i},\xi} , and each fluctuation of density is just 1/|\Lambda| . On the other hand, such fluctuation only happens on the boundary layer of \Lambda , whose order is dominated by the volume order in ( 1.25 ). Similar argument actually has already appeared in the proof of ( 1.21 ) in [ 36 , Lemma 2.1] . Besides the quantitative homogenization in (1), we also need to calculate carefully the errors in (2) and (3), i.e. that from local equivalence of ensembles and the regularity of density. They are discussed in detail in Sections 6.2 and 6.1 , and then we justify the density-free corrector ( 1.34 ) in Section 6.3 . 1.2.4. Hydrodynamic Limit This part corresponds to the green block in Figure 1 . Overall, Section 7 reformulates the relative entropy argument in [ 36 ] , and integrates the elements from homogenization. As mentioned briefly in ( 1.19 ), Theorem 1.3 resolves well the rate in the term R(\rho;F_{N}) . It remains to analyze the error term Q_{N} . The error Q_{N}=Q^{\Omega_{1}}_{N}+Q^{LD}_{N}+Q^{En}_{N}+Q^{\Omega_{2}}_{N} has several sources, and most of them can be handled after careful investigation, but the term Q^{\Omega_{1}}_{N} also involves the homogenization. This term comes from the microscopic current in non-gradient model, which does not have a gradient form and yields a diverging factor under the scaling. To overcome this difficulty, we need to show that, under a large space-time domain, one can replace such a term by a well-behaving function of gradient form asymptotically. This technique is called the gradient replacement (see [ 36 , Theorem 3.2 and Lemma 3.4] and Section 7.3.3 ). To implement it, a standard approach is Varadhan’s lemma (see [ 78 , Theorem 5.2] and [ 36 , Theorem 4.1] ) which gives the characterization of closed forms defined on a configuration space. We observe a connection between the gradient replacement and the dual quantity employed in the renormalization approach (see Section 6.6 ), thus our method provides another new route for the non-gradient hydrodynamic limit avoiding Varadhan’s lemma. 1.3. Perspectives It is natural to ask how to extend the results in this paper to other non-gradient particles, including GSEP, the multi-type SSEP, etc. Among the four steps in Figure 1 , the blue block and the green block respectively require only the local equivalence of the ensemble and the relative entropy, which are also developed in other models. The main problem is to derive Theorem 1.5 in other settings. The general framework of renormalization approach is also quite robust as it works in several different statistical physics models [ 3 , 21 , 12 , 4 , 25 ] . The only issue is whether one can recover all the technical inputs in the red block in Figure 1 . They are quite standard in PDE setting, but face challenges in particle systems due to different microscopical behaviors. One major progress in this paper is the coarse-grained lifting, which is inspired by [ 3 ] in percolation setting. This is not only a technique, but also reveals the essentials of homogenization. Roughly, the homogenization means the diffusion is close to the Brownian motion. If we make its analogue, an interacting particle system should be homogenized to independent random walks. Then the lifting allows us to compare the functions defined in two systems. Meanwhile, the coarse-grained lifting in this paper still has some limitation, as it uses the specific rule of exclusion that at most 1 particle per site. For GSEP studied in [ 55 ] , since N particles are allowed on every site, then it is hard to find any one-parameter free particle system to implement the lifting. A possible approach is to further enlarger the space via a superposition of several free particle systems of different parameters. This extension is not obvious and requires a careful justification of the steps in Section 3 . The idea of coarse-graining is not new in conservative particle systems. In the literature, it usually indicates the convergence of local density in hydrodynamic limit. We believe it is also related to our argument in Section 3 , as the convergence of density plays role in Proposition 3.9 . We hope to clarify this connection, then establish a more robust coarse-graining argument to cover more examples in the future work. 1.4. Notations We end the introduction by a summary of notations. 1.4.1. Geometry We use |\cdot| to stand for the usual \ell^{2} -norm for the finite dimensional vector or matrices. Meanwhile, for any x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , we define (1.35) \displaystyle\dist(x,y):=\max\{|x_{1}-y_{1}|,|x_{2}-y_{2}|,\cdots,|x_{d}-y_{d}|\}. This also generalizes to \dist(x,\Lambda):=\inf_{y\in\Lambda}\dist(x,y) for every subset \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} . We denote by \Lambda_{L}:=(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{d}\cap{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} the hypercube of side length around L , where L\in\mathbb{R}_{+} is not necessarily an integer for the flexibility. For every m\in\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\cdots\} , we also denote by {\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}:=\left(-\frac{3^{m}}{2},\frac{3^{m}}{2}\right)^{d}\cap{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} the hypercube of side length 3^{m} . For any n,m\in\mathbb{N} such that n<m , we denote by \mathcal{Z}_{m,n}:=3^{n}{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\cap{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m} and \mathcal{Z}_{n}:=3^{n}{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} . Then using \sqcup as the disjoint union, we have the following partition (1.36) \displaystyle{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}=\bigsqcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}), which provides convenience to implement the renormalization. For any finite set \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , we denote by |\Lambda| the number of vertices (1.37) \displaystyle|\Lambda|:=\#\{x:x\in\Lambda\}, and define the diameter as (1.38) \displaystyle\diam(\Lambda):=\max\{|x-y|:x,y\in\Lambda\}. We also define \partial\Lambda as the boundary set of \Lambda that (1.39) \displaystyle\partial\Lambda:=\{x\in\Lambda:\exists y\notin\Lambda,y\sim x\}, and denote by \Lambda^{-} the interior of \Lambda (1.40) \displaystyle\Lambda^{-}:=\Lambda\setminus\partial\Lambda. Recall that the set of bonds of \Lambda is defined as \Lambda^{*} in ( 1.1 ). We define its enlarged version (1.41) \displaystyle\overline{\Lambda^{*}}:=\{\{x,y\}:x\in\Lambda,y=x+e_{i},i=1,2,\cdots,d\}, where e_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} is the i -th directed unit vector, and also denote by \Lambda^{+} the vertices concerned in ( 1.41 ) (1.42) \displaystyle\Lambda^{+}:=\Lambda\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}(\Lambda+e_{i}). One motivation is that, for n,m\in\mathbb{N} such that n<m , despite of ( 1.36 ), we observe that \bigcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n})^{*}\subsetneq({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})^{*} . On the other hand, ( 1.41 ) provides a better partition structure for bonds (1.43) \displaystyle\overline{({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})^{*}}=\bigsqcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\overline{(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n})^{*}}. For disjoint sets \Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{\prime}=\emptyset , we define (\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})^{*} as the set of bonds between \Lambda and \Lambda^{\prime} (1.44) \displaystyle(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})^{*}:=\{\{x,y\}:x\in\Lambda,\ y\in\Lambda^{\prime}\}. Especially, (\Lambda,\Lambda^{c})^{*} is the set of bonds connecting \Lambda and its complement. 1.4.2. Probability spaces For every \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , we denote by \mathscr{F}_{\Lambda} the \sigma -algebra generated by the (\eta_{x})_{x\in\Lambda} and we write \mathscr{F} short for \mathscr{F}_{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} . Given \rho\in(0,1) as the density of particle, and make use of \mathbb{P}_{\rho} as the Bernoulli product measure \operatorname{Ber}(\rho)^{\otimes{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} on \mathcal{X} , thus (\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}_{\rho}) is the triplet of probability space most used in this paper. For the expectation under \mathbb{P}_{\rho} , we use the notation \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho} or \mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\cdot] . We make use of \mathbb{P}_{\rho,\Lambda},\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho,\Lambda} when we restrict our measure on (\eta_{x})_{x\in\Lambda} . We also denote by \mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N,\zeta} and \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N,\zeta} the probability and expectation under the canonical ensemble, i.e. N particles distributed uniformly on different sites of \Lambda with the configuration \zeta on \Lambda^{c} . We sometimes omit \zeta and just write them as \mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N} and \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N} . See also the notation \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda} defined in ( 3.14 ) about the canonical ensemble. 1.4.3. Function spaces For every 1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty , we denote by \left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{L^{p}} or \left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{p} the L^{p} norm over the probability space (\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}_{\rho}) , and denote by L^{p}(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}_{\rho}) or shortly L^{p} the set of random variables with finite norm. For any \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , let \mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda) be the set of \mathscr{F}_{\Lambda} -measurable local functions. We also define the Sobolev norm H^{1}(\Lambda) for a measurable function: (1.45) \displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Lambda)}=\left\langle{f^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. For every local functions, we can calculate its H^{1}(\Lambda) norm, and we also use H^{1}(\Lambda) to represent the set of functions with finite H^{1}(\Lambda) norm. Despite of the natural definition of \mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda) , the function space \mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) is the proper analogue of the function space H^{1}_{0} in the domain \Lambda . To see this, we can verify the following identity easily (1.46) \displaystyle\forall\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}),\qquad\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda)}=\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})}. This is the important extension property of H^{1}_{0} function, but a general \mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda) function does not necessarily satisfy it. We will not use the notation H^{1}_{0}(\Lambda) in the paragraphs for the conciseness of notation, while we keep in mind that \mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) plays the same role. Viewing the discussion above, we define the space of harmonic functions with respect to the Kawasaki dynamics (1.47) \displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\Lambda):=\{u\in H^{1}(\Lambda):\forall v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}),\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=0\}. Note that u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) does not imply that u\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda) and it can have dependence on the configuration outside \Lambda . 1.4.4. Operators The translation operator, exchange operator and Kawasaki operator are respectively defined in ( 1.6 ), ( 1.7 ), ( 1.1 ) and ( 1.5 ). For \eta\in\mathcal{X} and \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , we define (\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda) as the configuration restricted on \Lambda that (1.48) \displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\qquad(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda)_{x}:=\eta_{x}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in\Lambda\right\}}. We sometimes identify \eta\in\mathcal{X} as \eta=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\eta_{x}\delta_{x} for the convenience to manipulate. The affine function defined by (1.49) \displaystyle\ell_{p}(\eta):=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}(p\cdot x)\,\eta_{x}, is just a formal sum as there are infinite terms, while \pi_{b}\ell_{p} is well-defined as (1.50) \displaystyle\forall b=\{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*},\qquad(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})(\eta):=p\cdot(y-x)(\eta_{x}-\eta_{y}). A rigorous version of ( 1.49 ) is a sum restricted on the finite set \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} (1.51) \displaystyle\ell_{p,\Lambda}(\eta):=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}(p\cdot x)\,\eta_{x}. In Kawasaki dynamics, we define the tangent field along the direction e_{i} at x for u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} as (1.52) \displaystyle\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u:=(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}}). Some simple calculation gives us (1.53) \displaystyle(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}})(\eta)=\left(u(\eta^{x,x+e_{i}})-u(\eta)\right)(\eta_{x}-\eta_{x+e_{i}}), so the term is non-zero if and only if (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,0) or (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(0,1) . Moreover, for both two non-zero cases, they evaluate the change of u that a particle jumps from x to x+e_{i} . Similarly, we define the gradient field of u at x as (1.54) \displaystyle\nabla_{x}u:=(\nabla_{x,e_{1}}u,\nabla_{x,e_{2}}u,\cdots,\nabla_{x,e_{d}}u). For every p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}} , we also obtain that (1.55) \displaystyle p\cdot\nabla_{x}u=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{p}). The Glauber operator appears naturally in some steps of analysis. We denote by \eta^{x} the flip operator at x that \displaystyle(\eta^{x})_{z}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\eta_{z},&\qquad z\neq x;\\ 1-\eta_{z},&\qquad z=x.\end{array}\right. Then the Glauber derivative for f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} is defined by (1.58) \displaystyle\pi_{x}f:=f(\eta^{x})-f(\eta). Clearly, (\pi_{x}f)^{2} is independent of \eta_{x} . 1.4.5. Constants We usually use C to represent a positive finite constant and C(\cdots) to indicate its dependence on other parameters. The value of C may change from line to line. The following constants will be fixed and used throughout the paper. • d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}=\{1,2,3,\cdots\} for the dimension of lattice. • \lambda for the upper bound of the jump rate, i.e. c_{b}(\eta)\leqslant\lambda for any b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*},\eta\in\mathcal{X} . • \mathbf{r} for the radius of dependence of jump rate as indicated in Hypothesis 1.1 . 2. Analytic tools In this part, we collect all the necessary analytic tools in this paper. The two main results of this section are the modified Caccioppoli inequality (Proposition 2.6 ), and the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality (Proposition 2.13 and then Proposition 3.9 ). 2.1. Analytic tools on Kawasaki dynamics 2.1.1. Glauber operator meets Kawasaki operator Our first inequality comes from the observation in [ 50 , eq.(B.2)] , which states that we can exchange the site of the Glauber derivative by paying the error of the Kawasaki operator. Lemma 2.1 . Recall the L^{2} function space defined in Section 1.4.3 , then we have (2.1) \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\left\|{\pi_{x,y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}. Proof. As we know, (\pi_{x}f)^{2} does not depend on \eta_{x} , thus we decompose \left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}} with respect to the state of \eta_{y} \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}=\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{y}=1\right\}}+\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{y}=0\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}. We denote by \widetilde{\eta}=(\eta_{z})_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\setminus\{x,y\}} and F(\eta_{x},\eta_{y},\widetilde{\eta})=f(\eta) . Then we have \left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\\ =\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}\rho(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-\rho)(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}. We apply the triangle inequality for this norm. The trick is that we only replace the term involving \eta_{x}\neq\eta_{y} . For example, in the terms (F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2} , we add an intermediate term F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}) . This follows exactly the spirit of the Kawasaki operator \pi_{x,y} and we obtain \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}} \displaystyle\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}\rho(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-\rho)(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\qquad+\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}\rho(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-\rho)(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}\rho(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-\rho)(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\qquad+\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}. We notice the identity in the last equation \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}} \displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}\rho(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-\rho)(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}, \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x,y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}} \displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}2\rho(1-\rho)(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}, then we conclude the desired result. ∎ 2.1.2. Spectral inequality The spectral inequality is an important tool to analyze Markov processes. In this part, we summarize several spectral inequalities from the literature. The most used spectral inequality is the one for independent random variables known as Efron–Stein inequality. Here we state it and its reverse version. Lemma 2.2 (Efron–Stein inequality) . Let (X_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n} be i.i.d. random variables on E . (Then they can be regarded as random variables on E^{n} .) For any function f=f(X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n}) , we denote by (2.2) \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(i)}[\cdot]:=\int_{E}(\cdot)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{X_{i}},\qquad\mathbb{E}_{(-i)}[\cdot]:=\int_{E^{n-1}}(\cdot)\,\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,j\neq i}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{X_{j}}, and \mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(i)},\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(-i)} for the corresponding variances. Then we have (2.3) \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(i)}[\mathbb{E}_{(-i)}[f]]\leqslant\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}[f]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{(-i)}[\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(i)}[f]]. Proof. The upper bound is the classical Efron–Stein inequality, and one can find its proof in [ 18 , Theorem 3.1] . The lower bound, which could be seen as a reverse Efron–Stein inequality, is less well-known, but follows exactly the same strategy of proof. The authors learn the lower bound at first in [ 77 ] . ∎ A direct corollary of Efron–Stein inequality is the spectral inequality of the Glauber operator ( 1.4.4 ) under product Bernoulli measure. Corollary 2.3 (Spectral inequality for Glauber dynamics) . For any \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , we have (2.4) \displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho,\Lambda}[f]\leqslant\chi(\rho)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho,\Lambda}. Proof. We apply the classical Efron–Stein inequality and the upper bound is the right-hand side of ( 2.4 ). ∎ With some more treatment, we can also obtain the spectral inequality for the Kawasaki operator ( 1.5 ) under product Bernoulli measure. Lemma 2.4 . For any bounded set \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) , we have (2.5) \displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f]\leqslant\diam(\Lambda)^{2}\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{b}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Proof. We apply at first the spectral inequality for Glauber dynamics (2.6) \displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f]\leqslant\chi(\rho)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. We fix a direction in the canonical basis e_{i} , then for every x\in\Lambda , there exists a positive integer \ell depending on x , \displaystyle\ell(x):=\min\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}:x+ke_{i}\in\partial\Lambda\}. Then we apply Lemma 2.1 \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{x+e_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\left\|{\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}. We sum this inequality along the path x\to x+e_{i}\to x+2e_{i}\cdots\to x+\ell(x)e_{i} \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{x+\ell(x)e_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\|{\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}. Notice that x+\ell(x)e_{i}\in\partial\Lambda and f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) , so f does not depend on \eta_{x+\ell(x)e_{i}} and \pi_{x+\ell(x)e_{i}}f=0 . This implies (2.7) \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\|{\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}. We put ( 2.7 ) back to ( 2.6 ) and apply Cauchy–Schwarz inequality \displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f] \displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\ell(x)\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\langle{(\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\diam(\Lambda)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\langle{(\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Here the factor \chi(\rho) in ( 2.7 ) and ( 2.6 ) compensates, and we also make use of the fact \ell(x)\leqslant\diam(\Lambda) . We now exchange the order of the sum \sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)} \displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f]\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\diam(\Lambda)\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda:\exists j\in\mathbb{N}_{+},x+je_{i}\in b}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Because every bond b can be counted at most \diam(\Lambda) times along the direction e_{i} , we obtain the desired result. ∎ In [ 62 , Theorem 1] , Lu and Yau proved a generalized version of the spectral inequality for the Glauber dynamics. We do not need that one in this paper, but we will make use of [ 62 , Theorem 2] , the spectral inequality for Kawasaki dynamics under canonical ensemble. This sharp spectral gap estimate is also one of the key estimates in Varahdan’s method. Lemma 2.5 (Theorem 2, [ 62 ] ) . There exists a positive constant C=C(d) , such that for any L\in\mathbb{N}_{+} and any N\in\mathbb{N}_{+},N\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}| , we have (2.8) \displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\Lambda_{L},N}[f]\leqslant CL^{2}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},N}. 2.1.3. Modified Caccioppoli inequality In PDE setting, for the elliptic equation \displaystyle-\nabla\cdot\mathbf{a}(x)\nabla v=0,\qquad\text{ in }B_{r}, with uniform ellipticity |\xi|^{2}\leqslant\xi\cdot\mathbf{a}\xi\leqslant\lambda|\xi|^{2} , then v satisfies the classical Caccioppoli inequality \displaystyle\strokedint_{B_{r/2}}|\nabla v|^{2}\leqslant\frac{C(\lambda)}{r^{2}}\strokedint_{B_{r}}v^{2}. This inequality is also one key step to establish the quantitative homogenization, but was missing in the literature for infinite particle systems. A modified Caccioppoli inequality is proved in [ 38 , Proposition 3.9] , which differs from the classical one by an extra term on the right-hand side; see ( 2.10 ). Here we present its version in Kawasaki dynamics. The conditional expectation operator will be used in the following paragraphs. Recall \mathscr{F}_{\Lambda} defined in Section 1.4.2 . For \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and f\in L^{1} , we define (2.9) \displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda}f:=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[f\,|\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}\right]. Concretely, using the restriction operator defined in ( 1.48 ), it is calculated as \displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda}f(\eta)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda+\eta^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda^{c})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\eta^{\prime}). We usually denote by {\mathsf{A}}_{L}f\equiv{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{L}}f for short. Proposition 2.6 (Modified Caccioppoli inequality) . There exists {\theta(d,\lambda)\in(0,1)} , finite positive constants C(d,\lambda) , and R_{0}(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r}) such that for every L\geqslant R_{0} and {u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{3L})} (defined in ( 1.47 )), we have (2.10) \displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{L+2\mathbf{r}}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{L+2\mathbf{r}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\frac{CL^{-2}}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{u^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{\theta}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{3L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Its proof is similar to [ 38 , Proposition 3.9] , which can be summarized in the following three steps. (1) Test the harmonic function {u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{3L})} with its cutoff version {\mathsf{A}}_{L}u . (2) Obtain the L^{2} -term using the quadratic variation of the martingale ({\mathsf{A}}_{n}u)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}} . (3) Bootstrap the result from its weak version with a correct normalization volume factor. The first and second step can be seen as the main difference between particle system and PDE setting, where we make the cutoff in order to reduce the influence of particles from the boundary, and we also need the nice L^{2} -isometry of martingale to recover the L^{2} term of u . In Kawasaki dynamics, the quadratic variation structure is less obvious compared to the setting of continuous configuration space in [ 38 ] . Let us make some explicit calculation at first. It is clear that (2.11) \begin{split}\forall b\in(\Lambda_{n})^{*},\qquad&\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f={\mathsf{A}}_{n}\pi_{b}f,\\ \forall b\in(\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*},\qquad&\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f=0.\end{split} That is to say, the operators {\mathsf{A}}_{n} and \pi_{b} are commutative when the bond b stays in (\Lambda_{n})^{*} , and the influence is 0 when b is outside \Lambda_{n} . When b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*} , the situation is subtle as we will see the perturbation \displaystyle\forall x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x,\qquad\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta)={\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x,y})-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta). Since we apply the conditional expectation, the information of \eta_{x} is no longer accessible in {\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x,y}) and we have \displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x,y})={\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(\Lambda_{n}\setminus\{x\})+\eta_{y}\delta_{x}). Thus, near the boundary the Kawasaki operator is like resampling the state at x and we have (2.12) \displaystyle\forall x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x,\qquad\left\langle{(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=2\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. By the spectral inequality ( 2.4 ), we know that \displaystyle\chi(\rho)\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n}}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\geqslant\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n-2}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. The inequality is not on the desired direction, because we hope to give an upper bound for the boundary perturbation. For this reason, we would like to study how to control this Glauber derivative near the boundary at first. Lemma 2.7 . For {\mathsf{A}}_{n}\equiv{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}} defined in ( 2.9 ) and f\in L^{2} , the following estimate holds (2.13) \displaystyle\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant 4\left(\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). Proof. The left-hand side can be expressed with ( 2.12 ) (2.14) \begin{split}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}&=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}\left\langle{(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}2\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} We notice that (2.15) \begin{split}\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta)&={\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x})-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta)\\ &=\int_{\{0,1\}}\left({\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f(\eta^{x})-{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f(\eta)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\eta_{y})\\ &=\int_{\{0,1\}}\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\eta_{y}).\end{split} Using Jensen’s inequality, we have (2.16) \displaystyle\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} Then we apply Lemma 2.1 to {\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f and obtain that (2.17) \displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\left\|{\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}. We put ( 2.17 ) and ( 2.16 ) back to ( 2.14 ), and obtain that (2.18) \sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ \leqslant\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}4\chi(\rho)\left(\left\langle{\left(\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). For the first term on right-hand side, it is exactly the fluctuation on \partial\Lambda_{n+2} , so we apply the reverse Efron–Stein inequality (the first inequality of ( 2.2 )) to {\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f under the expectation over the \{\eta_{y}\}_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{n+2}} \displaystyle\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}} \displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[\mathbb{E}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[{\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f\,|\eta_{y}]] \displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[{\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f] \displaystyle=\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}. Recall that \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}} is defined in Section 1.4.2 , and note that \chi(\rho) also appears similarly in ( 2.4 ) in a reversed inequality. We also use the identity \mathbb{E}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[{\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f\,|\eta_{y}]={\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f here. Then we take the expectation of other variables to yield the estimate of the first term on the right-hand sideof ( 2.18 ) \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}4\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant 4\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. For the second term on the right-hand sideof ( 2.18 ), we apply ( 2.11 ) and once again Jensen’s inequality to obtain that \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}\left\langle{\left({\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}\pi_{x,y}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. This concludes the desired result. ∎ Remark 2.8 . The proof above is inspired by the example in [ 38 , Proposition 3.9] and [ 50 , Lemma 6.1, eq.(B.2)] , while similar arguments also appeared before in the intermediate steps in Varadhan’s characterization of closed form (see [ 54 , Lemma 4.15, Appendix A.4] ). Once we develop Lemma 2.7 , the rest of the proof follows that in [ 38 , Proposition 3.9] . Proof of Proposition 2.6 . The proof can be divided into three steps. Step 1: construction of test function. In the first step, we do some preparation. Our object is to regularize u such that it becomes a function in \mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda_{3L}^{-}) . A very natural idea is to apply the conditional expectation operator ( 2.9 ), then the information outside 3L will be averaged. In order to make this cutoff more smooth, we propose the following regularized version (2.19) \displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}f:=\frac{1}{\ell}\int_{0}^{\ell}{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t. Recall \Lambda_{L} is defined for L\in\mathbb{R}_{+} , so {\mathsf{A}}_{s+t} does for s,t\in\mathbb{R}_{+} . Its Kawasaki derivative can be calculated using ( 2.11 ) \displaystyle\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}f=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}f&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*};\\ \frac{1}{\ell}\int_{\tau(b)+}^{s+\ell}{\mathsf{A}}_{t}\pi_{b}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\frac{(\tau_{b}-s)\wedge 1}{\ell}\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau_{b}}f\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}&\text{if }b\in\left(\Lambda_{s},\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c}\right)^{*};\\ 0&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c})^{*}.\end{array}\right. Here the notation \tau(b) is defined as (2.23) \displaystyle\tau(b):=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\}. From the definition of hypercube in Section 1.4.1 , we know b\in\Lambda_{\tau(b)+} but b\notin\Lambda_{\tau(b)} . We will also make use of the following operator (2.24) \displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}f:=({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\circ{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell})(f)=\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t){\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t, The motivation comes from the following identity that (2.25) \displaystyle\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{f(\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}f)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t. Similar to ( 2.1.3 ), we also calculate its Kawasaki derivative as preparation (2.26) \pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}f=\\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}f&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*};\\ \frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{\tau(b)+}^{s+\ell}(s+\ell-t){\mathsf{A}}_{t}\pi_{b}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t&\\ \qquad\qquad+\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{(\tau(b)-2)\vee s}^{\tau(b)}(s+\ell-t)\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau(b)}f\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}&\text{if }b\in\left(\Lambda_{s},\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c}\right)^{*};\\ 0&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c})^{*}.\end{array}\right. Step 2: weak Caccioppoli inequality. We then prove the weak Caccioppoli inequality at first. Fix \theta^{\prime}(\lambda):=\frac{10\lambda}{1+10\lambda}\in(0,1) ; recall that \lambda\geqslant 1 is the constant in Hypothesis 1.1 . For every L>0 , s\geqslant L+2\mathbf{r},\ell>1,s+\ell<3L and {u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{s+\ell+2})} , we claim that (2.27) \ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ \leqslant\theta^{\prime}\left(\ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{s+\ell}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). The main idea is to use the conditional expectation \widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u given in ( 2.24 ), because it provides a cutoff that \widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda_{s+\ell+2}^{-}) . Then we test it with u \displaystyle 0=\left\langle{\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u(-\mathcal{L}_{s+\ell+2}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell+2})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II}+\mathbf{III}. Here decompose the right-hand side into the sum of three terms (2.28) \begin{split}\mathbf{I}&:=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho},\\ \mathbf{II}&:=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho},\\ \mathbf{III}&:=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho},\end{split} and we have the estimate (2.29) \displaystyle|\mathbf{I}|\leqslant|\mathbf{II}|+|\mathbf{III}|. The term \mathbf{I} is the easiest one to treat (2.30) \begin{split}\mathbf{I}&=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u){\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}(c_{b}\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} From the first line to the second line, we use the fact \widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}={\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\circ{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell} and the reversibility of {\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell} . From the second line to the third line, we use the fact c_{b} is \mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{s}} -measurable when b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*} and {\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}=\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell} from ( 2.26 ). The identity ( 2.30 ) does not apply directly to \mathbf{II} , because for b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*} , the jump rate c_{b} is no longer \mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{s}} -measurable. Therefore, we make use of the exact expression ( 2.24 ) (2.31) \begin{split}|\mathbf{II}|&=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left|\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right|\\ &\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\frac{\lambda}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}u)^{2}+(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\ &\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\frac{2\lambda}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\ &=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\lambda\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} From the first line to the second line, we make use of Young’s inequality and c_{b}\leqslant\lambda . From the second line to the third line, {\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}=\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell} and \left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} is also applied thanks to Jensen’s inequality. The term \mathbf{III} has two integrals following ( 2.26 ), which can be noted respectively by \mathbf{III.1} and \mathbf{III.2} . The first part is similar to ( 2.31 ) (2.32) \begin{split}|\mathbf{III.1}|&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\left|\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{\tau(b)+}^{s+\ell}(s+\ell-t)\left\langle{c_{b}({\mathsf{A}}_{t}\pi_{b}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right|\\ &\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\lambda\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} The second part is the key to make appear the L^{2} term \begin{split}|\mathbf{III.2}|&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\left|\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{(\tau(b)-2)\vee s}^{\tau(b)}(s+\ell-t)\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau(b)}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right|\\ &\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\frac{2\lambda}{\ell}\left\langle{\gamma^{-1}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau(b)}u)^{2}+\gamma(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}.\end{split} From the first line to the second line, Young’s inequality is applied with \gamma>0 to be fixed, together with the trivial bound (s+\ell-t)\leqslant\ell for t defied above. We rearrange the sum, and obtain (2.33) \begin{split}|\mathbf{III.2}|&\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\ell\rfloor-2}\sum_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c}\right)^{*}}\frac{2\lambda}{\ell}\left\langle{\gamma^{-1}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}u)^{2}+\gamma(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\ell\rfloor-2}\left(\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+n+2}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s+n}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left(\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}+\frac{2\gamma\lambda}{\ell}\right)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right)\\ &=\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\ell\rfloor-2}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left(\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}+\frac{2\gamma\lambda}{\ell}\right)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} Here we insert the estimate from Lemma 2.7 in the second line to handle the perturbation of boundary term, and then make use of the orthogonal decomposition of martingale from the second line to the third line. We choose \gamma=\ell , and put ( 2.30 ), ( 2.31 ), ( 2.32 ) and ( 2.33 ) back to ( 2.29 ), which concludes that (2.34) \sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ \leqslant 10\lambda\left(\ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). Then Widman’s filling-hole argument applies by adding 10\lambda\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} on the two sides (Jensen’s inequality is also applied to the right-hand side) (2.35) (1+10\lambda)\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ \leqslant 10\lambda\left(\ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). We note the martingale property of ({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)_{s\geqslant 0} and divide (1+10\lambda) on the two sides to obtain ( 2.27 ) with \theta^{\prime}=\frac{10\lambda}{1+10\lambda} . Step 3: bootstrap.
