When we take L large enough, \ell=L , and s=2L in ( 2.27 ), we obtain (2.36) \displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{2L}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{2L}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant 3^{d}\theta^{\prime}\left(\frac{L^{-2}}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{3L}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{3L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). The factor before the term \left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{3L}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} is correct, but an additional volume factor is added before \left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{3L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho} compared to the desired result ( 2.10 ). This is not good as we do not necessarily have 3^{d}\theta^{\prime}<1 . However, if we choose carefully s=(1+\delta)L and \ell=\delta L , we obtain (2.37) \frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{L}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{L}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ \leqslant(1+2\delta)^{d}\theta^{\prime}\left(\frac{(\delta L)^{-2}}{|\Lambda_{(1+2\delta)L}|}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{(1+2\delta)L}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{(1+2\delta)L}|}\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{(1+2\delta)L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). We can choose \delta small such that (1+2\delta)^{d}\theta^{\prime}<1 , and then iterate the Dirichlet energy term on the right-hand side, such that the domain increases progressively to \Lambda_{3L} . See [ 38 , eq.(56)-(60)] for details, since this step is an algebraic iteration and independent of model. ∎ 2.2. Analytic tools on independent particles In this part, we develop the analytic tools on independent particles. We denote by \widetilde{\mathcal{X}}:=\mathbb{N}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} for the configuration space of independent particles on {\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , which does not have constraint for the number of particles on every site. For \widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}} such that \widetilde{\eta}_{x}\geqslant 1 , we define the jump operator \displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}^{x,y}:=\widetilde{\eta}-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y}, and \displaystyle(\widetilde{\pi}_{x,y}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta}):=\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}^{x,y})-\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}) for any function \widetilde{u} on \widetilde{\mathcal{X}} . The generator of the independent particles is (2.38) \displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta}):=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\sum_{y\sim x}\widetilde{\pi}_{x,y}\widetilde{u}. This generates a dynamic that every particle jumps independently with rate 1 to the nearest neighbor site. Fix an \alpha>0 , we denote by \text{Poi}(\alpha) the Poisson distribution on \mathbb{N} with mean \alpha and \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}:=\text{Poi}(\alpha)^{\otimes{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} the probability measure on \widetilde{\mathcal{X}} which is stationary with respect to \widetilde{\mathcal{L}} in ( 2.38 ), and \left\llangle{\cdot}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} its associated expectation. We usually write \widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}} as a canonical random variable sampled under \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha} . We also denote by \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\Lambda,N} and \left\llangle{\cdot}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda,N} for the probability and expectation under the canonical ensemble, i.e. N particles distributed independently and uniformly in \Lambda . 2.2.1. Mecke’s identity The first lemma is about Mecke’s identity, which simplifies some expectation under \left\llangle{\cdot}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} by adding one additional particle. This identity is inspired from the reference [ 59 , Theorem 4.1] . Lemma 2.9 (Mecke’s identity) . For any \alpha>0 , any x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and any measurable function F:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R} . If \widetilde{\eta}_{x}F is integrable under \mathbb{P}_{\alpha} , then the following identity holds (2.39) \displaystyle\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}F(\widetilde{\eta})}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}=\alpha\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x})}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}. Proof. We make the calculation directly \displaystyle\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}F(\widetilde{\eta})}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle=e^{-\alpha}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{k}}{k!}\left\llangle{kF(\widetilde{\eta})\,|\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=k}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle=\alpha e^{-\alpha}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{(k-1)}}{(k-1)!}\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta})\,|\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=k}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle=\alpha e^{-\alpha}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{(k-1)}}{(k-1)!}\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x})\,|\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=k-1}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle=\alpha\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x})}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}. ∎ The calculation over the independent particle system has a close connection with the finite difference operator on lattice {\mathbb{Z}^{d}} or \mathbb{T}^{d}_{L} . Given a function \widetilde{u}:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R} which only depends on the configuration in \Lambda , and using the expression \widetilde{\eta}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}} , then we have the following canonical projection u_{N}:\Lambda^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} written as (2.40) \displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{N}):=\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}), where \widetilde{\eta}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}} . Moreover, (\widetilde{u}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}} are glued together as a function \widetilde{u} on \mathbb{N}^{\Lambda} if and only if \widetilde{u}_{N} is invariant under permutation for all N\in\mathbb{N}_{+} ; see [ 38 , Lemma A.1] for similar discussions. We state some more properties using the expression ( 2.40 ). To better treat the high dimensional function, we define the following notation for shorthand, \displaystyle\strokedint_{\Lambda^{N}}\left(\cdot\right):=\frac{1}{|\Lambda|^{N}}\sum_{x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}\in\Lambda}\left(\cdot\right), then using the notation ( 2.40 ), we observe (2.41) \displaystyle\left\llangle{\widetilde{u}}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda,N}=\strokedint_{\Lambda^{N}}\widetilde{u}_{N}. For any integer 1\leqslant i\leqslant N and e\in U:=\{\pm e_{1},\pm e_{2},\cdots,\pm e_{d}\} , the finite difference operator \mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e} is defined for \widetilde{u}_{N} as (2.42) \displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\widetilde{u}_{N})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}):=\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i}+e,\cdots,x_{N})-\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i},\cdots,x_{N}), which is commutative in the sense (2.43) \displaystyle\forall 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N,\forall e,e^{\prime}\in U,\qquad\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}=\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}. Combing the canonical projection ( 2.40 ), the finite difference operator is related to the generator \widetilde{\mathcal{L}} in ( 2.38 ) by the following identity (2.44) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}). Then a lot of analytic tools on Euclidean space can be applied to independent particles. 2.2.2. H^{2} -estimate In the following paragraphs, we will recall H^{2} estimate for independent particles (indeed identity), which will be used in the multiscale Poincaré estimate later in Proposition 2.13 . The proof follows [ 11 , Lemma B.19] and [ 17 , Proposition 3.10] after a careful review. We will also explain in detail in Remark 2.11 the difficulty met when developing the counterpart for Kawasaki dynamics. Lemma 2.10 (Dimension-free H^{2} estimate on torus) . For any L,N\in\mathbb{N}_{+} and any function u,f:(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}\to\mathbb{R} satisfying (2.45) \displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u=f, the following identity holds (2.46) \displaystyle\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\sum_{e,e^{\prime}\in U}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u)^{2}=4\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}f^{2}. Proof. It is easy to verify the identity \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}, so ( 2.45 ) is equivalent to \displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u=f. We evaluate the L^{2} sum of the two sides (2.47) \begin{split}\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}f^{2}&=\frac{1}{4}\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)^{2}\\ &=\frac{1}{4}\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},-e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right).\end{split} On torus, it is easy to verify the integration by part formula for u,v:(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}\to\mathbb{R} \displaystyle\forall 1\leqslant i\leqslant N,e\in U,\qquad\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u)v=\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}u(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}v). We apply it and the commutativity ( 2.43 ) to the right-hand side of ( 2.47 ) \displaystyle\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},-e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right) \displaystyle=\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},-e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right) \displaystyle=\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},-e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right) \displaystyle=\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right) \displaystyle=\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right)^{2}. We put it back to the right-hand side of ( 2.47 ) and conclude ( 2.46 ). ∎ Remark 2.11 . We give a more conceptual proof of H^{2} estimate on torus, so one notices the challenges facing exclusion. Consider the following equation on \mathbb{T}^{N} \displaystyle-\Delta u=f. Then we have the following calculation using the L^{2} estimate for the two sides \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}f^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}(-\Delta u)^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}\partial_{i}u\right)^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}(\partial_{i}\partial_{i}u)(\partial_{j}\partial_{j}u). Using the integration by part, then we have \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}(\partial_{i}\partial_{i}u)(\partial_{j}\partial_{j}u)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}u)^{2}. Then we conclude that \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}f^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}u)^{2}. Lemma 2.10 is just a discrete version of the above proof. If one hopes to recover a similar identity on Kawasaki dynamics for u,f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} , such that -\sum_{b\in(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{*}}\pi_{b}u=f , then we also have \pi_{b}=-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{b}\pi_{b} and integration by part formula. However, the main difficulty appears in the commutativity. The identity \displaystyle\pi_{b}\pi_{b^{\prime}}=\pi_{b^{\prime}}\pi_{b}, holds when b\cap b^{\prime}=\emptyset . Otherwise, when b,b^{\prime} shares common endpoint, as the symmetry group is not Abelian, some exotic term will generate and pose a challenge. We then extend the result above to the discrete Poisson equation on cube \Lambda_{L} . Here we add the Neumann boundary condition ( 2.48 ), and the indicator in ( 2.49 ) excludes the second-order finite difference outside \Lambda_{L}^{+} . Corollary 2.12 (Dimension-free H^{2} estimate on cube) . For any L,N\in\mathbb{N}_{+} and any function u,f:(\Lambda_{L})^{N}\to\mathbb{R} satisfying \displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u=f, with the Neumann boundary condition (2.48) \displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{i}\in\partial\Lambda_{L},x_{i}+e\notin\Lambda_{L}\right\}}=0, we have (2.49) \displaystyle\strokedint_{(\Lambda_{L})^{N}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\sum_{e,e^{\prime}\in U}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x_{i}=x_{j},e=e^{\prime},x_{i}+2e\notin\Lambda_{L}\right\}}=4\strokedint_{(\Lambda_{L})^{N}}f^{2}. Proof. The main idea of proof is periodization. Only in this proof, we work on half integers. With a translation and scaling, we denote by □ the cube of size L that {\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}=(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2})^{d}\cap[0,L]^{d} , and set \widetilde{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}=(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2})^{d}\cap[-L,L]^{d} . We still denote by u,f:({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}})^{N}\to\mathbb{R} and extend them to \widetilde{u},\widetilde{f}:(\widetilde{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}})^{N}\to\mathbb{R} by mirror symmetry (2.50) \displaystyle x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}\in(\widetilde{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}})^{N},\qquad\widetilde{u}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}):=u(|x_{1}|,\cdots,|x_{N}|). Next, we identify the opposite sides of the cube [-L,L]^{d} together to get a torus, and view \widetilde{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}} as a lattice torus \mathbb{T}^{d}_{2L} . Then functions \widetilde{u},\widetilde{f} on \widetilde{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}} can be regarded as functions on \mathbb{T}^{d}_{2L} . Moreover, the Neaumann boundary condition and the mirror symmetry implies that on the whole \mathbb{T}^{d}_{2L} , we have \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\widetilde{u}=\widetilde{f}. Therefore, Lemma 2.10 applies to obtain \displaystyle\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{2L})^{N}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\sum_{e,e^{\prime}\in U}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\widetilde{u})^{2}=4\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{2L})^{N}}\widetilde{f}^{2}. Each side counts 2^{d} times in the integration over □ , which gives us \displaystyle\strokedint_{{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}^{N}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\sum_{e,e^{\prime}\in U}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\widetilde{u})^{2}=4\strokedint_{{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}^{N}}\widetilde{f}^{2}. We realize that the second-order derivative of \widetilde{u} near the boundary vanishes due to the Neumann boundary condition and the mirror symmetry, then we conclude ( 2.49 ). ∎ 2.2.3. Multiscale Poincaré inequality In this part, we introduce the notion of spatial average and use it to develop some kind of multiscale Poincaré inequality. We define the gradient by adding one more particle (2.51) \begin{split}(\partial_{k}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta},x)&:=\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x+e_{k}})-\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}),\\ (\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta},x)&:=\left((\partial_{1}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta},x),(\partial_{2}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta},x),\cdots,(\partial_{d}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta},x)\right).\end{split} This is related to ( 2.42 ) by Lemma 2.9 and is particularly useful when we pass the gradient to the exclusion dynamics in the next section. Recalling the definition of the enlarged domain in ( 1.42 ), we define the filtration (2.52) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Lambda^{+}}:=\sigma\left(\sum_{x\in\Lambda^{+}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x},\{\widetilde{\eta}_{y},y\in(\Lambda^{+})^{c}\}\right). Using \mathcal{Z}_{m,n} and \mathcal{Z}_{n} defined in Section 1.4.1 , we also define the spatial average operator (2.53) \displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{n}(\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta},x):=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\left(\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{y\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\llangle{(\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta},y)\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n})^{+}}}\right\rrangle\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}\right\}}. That is, this operator makes spatial average over the added particle and over the local configuration. The enlarged domain (z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n})^{+} is needed to include all the sites where the particle in (\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta},x))_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}} is added (see ( 2.51 )). Then note that \left\llangle{\cdot\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n})^{+}}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} does not depend on \alpha>0 , so we drop the density \alpha . This leads to the multiscale Poincaré inequality. Proposition 2.13 (Multiscale Poincaré inequality) . There exists a finite positive constant C{=C(d)} such that for all bounded functions \widetilde{u}:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R} such that \left\llangle{\widetilde{u}\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+}}}\right\rrangle=0 , we have (2.54) \left\llangle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\widetilde{u}^{2}}\right\rrangle^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\alpha}\leqslant C\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\left\llangle{|\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta},x)|^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\ +C\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\llangle{\left|\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}\right|^{2}(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}. Proof. We are only interested in the case that \widetilde{u} is integrable under \left\llangle{\cdot}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} . The proof follows [ 38 , Proposition 3.5] and we give a sketch here. By Jensen’s inequality, we may replace u by \widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha}[u|\sigma(\widetilde{\eta}_{x})_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+}}] and assume u is a local function on {\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+} . Let \widetilde{w} solve the following equation: \displaystyle-\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\sum_{y\sim x}\widetilde{\pi}_{x,y}\widetilde{w}=-\widetilde{u}, in the sense of the Poisson equation with the same Neumann boundary condition ( 2.48 ) for \widetilde{w} in Corollary 2.12 . More precisely, we can fix the number of particles N in {\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+} and the information outside {\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+} . Then by the identity ( 2.44 ), the equation is written in the form of Corollary 2.12 , which is solvable recursively. This will give us the H^{2} estimate for \widetilde{w} using the projection. We have \displaystyle\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\left\llangle{\widetilde{u}^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle=\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(\widetilde{\pi}_{x,x+e_{i}}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\pi}_{x,x+e_{i}}\widetilde{w})}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\left\llangle{(\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w})(\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}. Here we use the Neumann boundary condition and Mecke’s identity in Lemma 2.9 . Then we add the local averages \displaystyle\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\left\llangle{\widetilde{u}^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\left\llangle{(\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}-\mathsf{S}_{0}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w})(\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}\frac{\alpha}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\left\llangle{(\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}-\mathsf{S}_{n+1}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w})(\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} \displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\alpha}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\left\llangle{(\mathsf{S}_{m}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\mathsf{S}_{m}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w})(\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}. Here we use the tower property of conditional expectations. We just focus on one scale, which gives us \displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}}\left\llangle{(\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}-\mathsf{S}_{n+1}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w})(\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}\right| \displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\llangle{(\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u})(\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}-\mathsf{S}_{n+1}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w})(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}\right| \displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\llangle{\left|\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}\right|^{2}(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\llangle{\left|\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}-\mathsf{S}_{n+1}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}\right|^{2}(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\leqslant C\alpha^{-\frac{1}{2}}3^{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\llangle{\left|\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}\right|^{2}(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\llangle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\widetilde{u}^{2}}\right\rrangle^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\alpha}. From the third line to the fourth line, we use the Poincaré inequality of independent particles for the term \left|\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}-\mathsf{S}_{n+1}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{w}\right|^{2} , and this will give the factor 3^{n} . The output is the second-order derivatives of \widetilde{w} , which will be bounded by \left\llangle{\widetilde{u}^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha} using the dimension-free H^{2} estimate in Corollary 2.12 . Here the factor \alpha^{-\frac{1}{2}} comes from another application of Mecke’s identity ( 2.39 ), and this concludes ( 2.54 ). ∎ 3. Coarse-grained lifting This part introduces the key technique to handle the constraint of particle numbers in Kawasaki dynamics, which is the coarse-grained lifting to independent particles. Let \widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{N}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} stand for the configuration of independent particles, and \eta\in\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} for the configuration with exclusion. We aim to embed \mathcal{X} into \widetilde{\mathcal{X}} , and a natural idea is to define the following projection []:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X} that (3.1) \displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\qquad[\widetilde{\eta}]_{x}:=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\geqslant 1\right\}}. Therefore, [\widetilde{\eta}] only indicates whether the site is occupied, but does not care about the exact number of particles. This projection operator also induces an extension for every function u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} by pull-back that (3.2) \displaystyle[u]:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad[u](\widetilde{\eta}):=u([\widetilde{\eta}]), and we call [u] the coarsened function of u . In the following paragraphs, we will use [\widetilde{\eta}] to represent the configuration with exclusion, and explore several identities under this projection/coarsen. 3.1. Grand canonical ensemble Let the configuration of independent particles \widetilde{\eta} follow the law \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha} , which is independent Poisson distribution of parameter \alpha>0 . In order to make [\widetilde{\eta}] of the same law as sampled from \mathbb{P}_{\rho} , we make a specific choice between the parameters such that e^{-\alpha}=1-\rho , i.e. (3.3) \displaystyle\forall\rho\in(0,1),\qquad\alpha(\rho):=-\log(1-\rho). Under this specific choice of parameter, we can see the coarsen function [u] as a lift of u thanks of the following proposition. Proposition 3.1 (Coarsen-grained lifting) . Given \rho\in(0,1) and \alpha(\rho) defined as ( 3.3 ) and \widetilde{\eta} sampled from \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)} , then [\widetilde{\eta}]\in\mathcal{X} follows the law \mathbb{P}_{\rho} . As a consequence, for every u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} integrable under \mathbb{P}_{\rho} , its coarsen function satisfies (3.4) \displaystyle\left\llangle{[u]}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}=\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Proof. With the choice of the parameter ( 3.3 ) and the definition of the projection operator ( 3.1 ), we have \displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\qquad\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}[[\widetilde{\eta}]_{x}=0] \displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}[\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=0]=e^{-\alpha(\rho)}=1-\rho, \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}[[\widetilde{\eta}]_{x}=1] \displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}[\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\geqslant 1]=1-\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}[\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=0]=\rho. Therefore, [\widetilde{\eta}]_{x} follows the Bernoulli law with parameter \rho . Since (\widetilde{\eta}_{x})_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} are i.i.d. random variables, [\widetilde{\eta}] has the same law as \mathbb{P}_{\rho} . Combing this fact and ( 3.2 ), the identity ( 3.4 ) is a direct corollary \displaystyle\left\llangle{[u]}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}=\left\llangle{u([\widetilde{\eta}])}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}=\left\langle{u(\eta)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. ∎ Although we can couple the static configuration between the two systems and obtain the nice identity ( 3.4 ), similar result cannot be extended to the Dirichlet energy and we cannot expect a similar identity like (3.5) \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sum_{y\in\Lambda,y\sim x}\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(\widetilde{\pi}_{x,y}[u])^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}=C(\rho)\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda,x\sim y}\left\langle{(\pi_{x,y}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. On the other hand, the coarse-grained lifting can be very useful when evaluating the spatial average of the gradient. We establish the following identity, which can be used as the gradient coupling. We recall the definition of the tangent field \nabla_{x,e_{i}} for Kawasaki dynamics defined in ( 1.52 ). Proposition 3.2 (Gradient coupling) . For every \rho\in(0,1),\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} , the following identity holds for every i\in\{1,\cdots,d\} (3.6) \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\widetilde{\pi}_{x,x+e_{i}}[u]}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}=\frac{\alpha(\rho)}{2\rho}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Proposition 3.2 is the result of the following lemmas. We state them separately as they can be useful in other proofs. The first lemma is a similar version of Mecke’s identity in Kawasaki dynamics. Here, instead of adding a particle, the added particle is understood as “forcing the site occupied”. Lemma 3.3 (Mecke’s identity in Kawasaki dynamics) . For every \rho\in(0,1) and u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} , the following identity holds (3.7) \displaystyle\left\langle{\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u}\right\rangle_{\rho}=2\rho\left(\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1}\right\rangle_{\rho}-\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). Proof. Recall the identity ( 1.53 ) and the term is non-zero if and only if (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,0) or (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(0,1) . Then we obtain that \displaystyle\left\langle{(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}})}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=2\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(0,1)\right\}}-u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,0)\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Here noticing that the term (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,1) is canceled on the right-hand side of the equation, we can rewrite it as \displaystyle\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(0,1)\right\}}-u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,0)\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(0,1)\right\}}+u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,1)\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle\qquad-\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,0)\right\}}+u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,1)\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1\right\}}-u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{x}=1\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Therefore, we obtain the identity \displaystyle\left\langle{(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}})}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=2\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1\right\}}-u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{x}=1\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=2\rho\left(\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1}\right\rangle_{\rho}-\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right). ∎ The second lemma makes the bridge between Lemma 2.9 and Lemma 3.3 , and is a corollary from Proposition 3.1 . Lemma 3.4 (Change of variable) . For any \rho\in(0,1) and x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} , let \eta\in\mathcal{X} be sampled from \mathbb{P}_{\rho} and \widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}} sampled from \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)} , then we have (3.8) \displaystyle([\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}],[\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{y}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}(\eta\vee\delta_{x},\eta\vee\delta_{y}), where \eta\vee\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{X} is defined as (\eta\vee\delta_{x})_{x}=\eta_{x}\vee 1 and (\eta\vee\delta_{x})_{z}=\eta_{z} for z\neq x . Proof. The proof follows the observation that \displaystyle[\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}]=[\widetilde{\eta}]\vee\delta_{x}. Thus, we apply Proposition 3.1 \displaystyle([\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}],[\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{y}])=([\widetilde{\eta}]\vee\delta_{x},[\widetilde{\eta}]\vee\delta_{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}(\eta\vee\delta_{x},\eta\vee\delta_{y}). ∎ Proof of Proposition 3.2 . We combine the results in Lemma 2.9 , Lemma 3.3 and Lemma 3.4 \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\widetilde{\pi}_{x,x+e_{i}}[u]}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)} \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq.Mecke}}}{{=}}\alpha(\rho)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\llangle{[u](\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x+e_{i}})-[u](\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x})}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)} \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq.CoarsenFunction}}}{{=}}\alpha(\rho)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\llangle{u([\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x+e_{i}}])-u([\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}])}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)} \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq.ChangeVariable}}}{{=}}\alpha(\rho)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{u(\eta\vee\delta_{x+e_{i}})-u(\eta\vee\delta_{x})}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq.GradientKawasaki}}}{{=}}\frac{\alpha(\rho)}{2\rho}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}})}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Here from the third line to the fourth line, we also use the fact \left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{u(\eta\vee\delta_{x})}\right\rangle_{\rho} . ∎ At the end of this subsection, we give another application of Lemma 3.3 . We recall the notation \Lambda^{-} defined in ( 1.40 ) and the gradient \nabla_{x} defined in ( 1.54 ). Corollary 3.5 . For every bounded set \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and u\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) , we have (3.9) \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}u}\right\rangle_{\rho}=0. Proof. We just focus on the gradient field along one direction e_{i} and apply ( 3.7 ) \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=2\rho\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left(\left\langle{u(\eta\vee\delta_{x+e_{i}})}\right\rangle_{\rho}-\left\langle{u(\eta\vee\delta_{x})}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right) \displaystyle=2\rho\left(\sum_{x\notin\Lambda,x-e_{i}\in\Lambda}\left\langle{u(\eta\vee\delta_{x})}\right\rangle_{\rho}-\sum_{x\in\Lambda,x-e_{i}\notin\Lambda}\left\langle{u(\eta\vee\delta_{x})}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right) \displaystyle=2\rho\left(\sum_{x\notin\Lambda,x-e_{i}\in\Lambda}\left\langle{u(\eta)}\right\rangle_{\rho}-\sum_{x\in\Lambda,x-e_{i}\notin\Lambda}\left\langle{u(\eta)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right) \displaystyle=0. In the first line, we also use the observation \left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{u(\eta\vee\delta_{x})}\right\rangle_{\rho} . Then the proof is similar to the discrete Stokes’ formula that all the terms except those on the boundary cancel, which yields the second line. The condition u\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) implies that u(\eta\vee\delta_{x})=u(\eta) and passes the result from the second line to the third line. Finally, as the terms in \sum_{x\in\Lambda,x+e_{i}\notin\Lambda} and \sum_{x\in\Lambda,x-e_{i}\notin\Lambda} are coupled, we obtain 0 . ∎ 3.2. Canonical ensemble We establish a gradient coupling similar to Proposition 3.2 under the canonical ensemble. Recall \Lambda^{+} defined in ( 1.42 ). Proposition 3.6 (Gradient coupling) . For every M\in\mathbb{N},\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} a \mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{+}} -measurable function, let (\widetilde{\eta}_{x})_{x\in\Lambda^{+}} be sampled from the canonical ensemble of M independent particles \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\Lambda^{+},M} and (P_{\Lambda,M,N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}} be the probability in N of the number of occupied sites defined as (3.10) \displaystyle P_{\Lambda,M,N}:=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\Lambda^{+},M}\left[\sum_{z\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{y\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{z}>0\right\}}=N-1\right],\qquad y\in\Lambda^{+}, then the following identity holds for every i\in\{1,\cdots,d\} (3.11) \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\llangle{\partial_{i}[u](\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda^{+},M}=\sum_{N=1}^{M+}\frac{|\Lambda^{+}|P_{\Lambda,M,N}}{2N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}. Remark 3.7 . One can check that ( 3.10 ) is well-defined and does not depend on the choice of y . One can pick a specific y and put it back to ( 3.11 ). This will make the identity there a little strange, but it transfers the symmetry used in the proof. To prove this proposition, we also give a Mecke’s identity like Lemma 3.3 under the canonical ensemble. Lemma 3.8 (Mecke’s identity under canonical ensemble) . Given u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} , then for every N\in\mathbb{N} , a finite subset \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and x\in\Lambda , the following identity holds (3.12) \displaystyle\left\langle{\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}=\frac{2N}{|\Lambda^{+}|}\left(\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}-\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}\right). Proof. The proof follows the similar strategy as Lemma 3.3 \displaystyle\left\langle{(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}})}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N} \displaystyle=2\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(0,1)\right\}}-u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,0)\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N} \displaystyle=2\left\langle{u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1\right\}}-u(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{x}=1\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N} \displaystyle=\frac{2N}{|\Lambda^{+}|}\left(\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}-\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}\right). Here from the first line to the second line, we use the fact that the case (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,1) cancels. From the second line to the third line, we notice that x,x+e_{i}\in\Lambda^{+} , so \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda^{+},N}[\eta_{x}=1]=\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda^{+},N}[\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1]=\frac{{(|\Lambda^{+}|-1)\choose(N-1)}}{{|\Lambda^{+}|\choose N}}=\frac{N}{|\Lambda^{+}|}. ∎ Proof of Proposition 3.6 . Notice that, for every x\in\Lambda^{+} , we have (3.13) \begin{split}&\left\llangle{[u](\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x})}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda^{+},M}\\ &=\left\llangle{u([\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}])}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda^{+},M}\\ &=\sum_{N=1}^{M+1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\Lambda^{+},M}\left[\sum_{z\in\Lambda^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{z}+\delta_{x}>0\right\}}=N\right]\left\llangle{u([\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}])\,\big|\sum_{z\in\Lambda^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{z}+\delta_{x}>0\right\}}=N}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda^{+},M}\\ &=\sum_{N=1}^{M+1}P_{\Lambda,M,N}\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}.\end{split} Here we apply the definition of the projection operator ( 3.1 ) from the first line to the second line, and the total probability formula from the second line to the third line. The passage from the third line to the fourth line requires some work. Firstly, we recall the identity ( 3.10 ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\Lambda^{+},M}\left[\sum_{z\in\Lambda^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{z}+\delta_{x}>0\right\}}=N\right]=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\Lambda^{+},M}\left[\sum_{z\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{x\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{z}>0\right\}}=N-1\right]=P_{\Lambda,M,N}. We also remark that P_{\Lambda,M,N} is well-defined and does not depend on the excluded site x . Secondly, we notice that, for every choice of vertex set V\subseteq\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{x\} such that |V|=N-1 , in the following set \displaystyle\left\{\sum_{z\in\Lambda^{+}}\widetilde{\eta}_{z}=M:\widetilde{\eta}_{z}>0\text{ for all }z\in V,\text{ and }\widetilde{\eta}_{z}=0\text{ for all }z\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus(\{x\}\cup V)\right\}, the number of configurations is the same. Therefore, the configuration [\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x}] contains a particle on x , and the other occupied positions are uniformly distributed conditioned on the number of occupied sites. This generalizes the coarse-grained lifting under the canonical ensemble, and gives the passage concerning the conditional expectation in the third line of ( 3.13 ). Applying ( 3.13 ), we obtain \displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\llangle{[u](\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x+e_{i}})-[u](\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x})}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda^{+},M} \displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{M+1}P_{\Lambda,M,N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left(\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x+e_{i}}=1}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}-\left\langle{u\,|\eta_{x}=1}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}\right) \displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{M+1}\frac{|\Lambda^{+}|P_{\Lambda,M,N}}{2N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}})}\right\rangle_{\Lambda^{+},N}. Here from the second line to the third line, we also use ( 3.12 ). This yields the desired result. ∎ 3.3. Weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality As the main result of this subsection, we combine the coarse-grained lifting and gradient coupling to obtain the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality on Kawasaki dynamics. Here we define \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}} similar to ( 2.52 ) (3.14) \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}:=\sigma\left(\sum_{x\in\Lambda^{+}}\eta_{x},\{\eta_{y},y\in(\Lambda^{+})^{c}\}\right). Recall the notations about the canonical ensemble in Section 1.4.2 , we actually have {\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N,\zeta}[\cdot]=\mathbb{P}_{\rho}[\cdot|\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}](N,\zeta)} for every \rho\in[0,1] . Proposition 3.9 (Weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality) . There exists a finite positive constant C=C(d) such that for all measurable functions u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} such that \left\langle{u\,|\mathcal{G}_{{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+}}}\right\rangle=0 , the following estimate is established (3.15) \left\langle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}u^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\ \leqslant C\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\mathbf{N}^{*}_{z,n}}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}u|\mathcal{G}_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}}}\right\rangle\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}, where the random variable \mathbf{N}_{z,n}^{*} is defined as \mathbf{N}_{z,n}^{*}(\eta):=\begin{cases}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}}\eta_{x},\ &\rho\leqslant\frac{1}{2},\\ \sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}}(1-\eta_{x}),\ &\rho>\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases} The integrand in the expectation is defined to be zero if \mathbf{N}_{z,n}^{*}=0 . Proof. Step 1: forward procedure. In order to better estimate its L^{2} norm, we need the multiscale Poincaré inequality ( 2.54 ). Since this is only established in independent particle systems, we make use of the coarse-grained lifting developed in Proposition 3.1 as a bridge, that is \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}u^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle=\left\llangle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}[u]^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\leqslant C\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}(\rho)\sum_{n=1}^{m}3^{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\llangle{\left|\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}[u]\right|^{2}(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}. We also remark that \left\langle{u\,|\mathcal{G}_{{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+}}}\right\rangle=0 ensures the condition \left\langle{[u]\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}^{+}}}\right\rangle=0 to apply ( 2.54 ). Step 2: backward procedure. We focus on one term \left\llangle{\left(\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}[u]\right)^{2}(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)} , which is the spatial average in the independent particles. Our main task in this step is to bring (\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}[u])(\widetilde{\eta},z) back to the original Kawasaki dynamics from the coarsen operator. We apply the definition of \mathsf{S}_{n} in ( 2.53 ) (3.16) \left\llangle{\left|\mathsf{S}_{n}\widetilde{\nabla}[u]\right|^{2}(\widetilde{\eta},z)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}\\ =\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}\left[\sum_{y\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}}\widetilde{\eta}_{y}=M\right]\left\llangle{\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\llangle{\widetilde{\nabla}[u](\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},M}\right|^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}. More precisely, by \left\llangle{\widetilde{\nabla}[u](\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},M} we mean \displaystyle\left\llangle{\widetilde{\nabla}[u](\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},M}=\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}\widetilde{\nabla}[u](\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})+\widetilde{\eta}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c},x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},M}(\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}). Then we apply the gradient coupling ( 3.11 ) to the term \widetilde{\eta}^{\prime} in the right-hand side \displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\llangle{\partial_{i}[u](\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},M}\right)^{2} \displaystyle=\left(\sum_{N=1}^{M+1}P_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},M,N}\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}\right. \displaystyle\qquad\left.\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{(\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u)(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})+[\widetilde{\eta}]\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c})}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},N}\right)^{2} \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{N=1}^{M+1}P_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},M,N}\left(\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}\right)^{2} \displaystyle\qquad\left(\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{(\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u)(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})+[\widetilde{\eta}]\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c})}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},N}\right)^{2}. From the second line to the third line, we make use of Jensen’s inequality. We also remark that, our function is not \mathcal{F}_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}} -measurable, thus we keep [\widetilde{\eta}]\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c} after the local average. We put this result back to ( 3.16 ) and apply Proposition 3.1 to [\widetilde{\eta}]\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c} , which gives us (3.17) \left\llangle{\left(\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{(\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u)(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})+[\widetilde{\eta}]\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c})}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},N}\right)^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}\\ =\underbrace{\left\langle{\left(\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},N}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}}_{=:F(N)}. Using the definition ( 3.10 ) with a specific choice y=z there, we have \displaystyle P_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},M,N}=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},M}\left[\sum_{x\in(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})\setminus\{z\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}>0\right\}}=N-1\right]. Then we apply Fubini’s lemma to the double sum \displaystyle\left\llangle{\left(\mathsf{S}_{n}(\partial_{i}[u])(\widetilde{\eta},z)\right)^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)} \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}\left[\sum_{y\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}}\widetilde{\eta}_{y}=M\right]\sum_{N=1}^{M+1}P_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},M,N}\left(\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}\right)^{2}F(N) \displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{M\geqslant N-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}\left[\sum_{y\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}}\widetilde{\eta}_{y}=M\right]\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},M}\left[\sum_{x\in(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})\setminus\{z\}}[\widetilde{\eta}]_{x}=N-1\right]\right)\left(\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}\right)^{2}F(N) \displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(\rho)}\left[\sum_{x\in(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})\setminus\{z\}}[\widetilde{\eta}]_{x}=N-1\right]\left(\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}\right)^{2}F(N) \displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{x\in(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})\setminus\{z\}}\eta_{x}=N-1\right]\left(\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}\right)^{2}F(N) \displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\tbinom{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|-1}{N-1}\rho^{N-1}(1-\rho)^{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|-N}\left(\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}\right)^{2}F(N) \displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2\rho}\tbinom{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{N}\rho^{N}(1-\rho)^{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|-N}\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}F(N) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\rho}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{x\in(z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})}\eta_{x}=N\right]\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2N}F(N). The identity from the third line to the fourth line comes from the definition of conditional probability. From the fourth line to the fifth line, we apply the coarse-grained lifting in Proposition 3.1 once again. We define the random variable {\mathbf{N}_{z,n}=\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}}\eta_{x}} to be the number of particles in the cube z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+} . Using this notation, the last term becomes \frac{1}{2\rho}\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}F(\mathbf{N}_{z,n})}\right\rangle_{\rho} , and it gives us (3.18) \left\llangle{\left(\mathsf{S}_{n}(\partial_{i}[u])(\widetilde{\eta},z)\right)^{2}}\right\rrangle_{\alpha(\rho)}\\ \leqslant\frac{1}{2\rho}\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}\left(\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{(\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u)}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. We remark that we define this integrand to be zero if \mathbf{N} is zero. Plugging this back, we have (3.19) \left\langle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}u^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\ \leqslant C\left(\frac{\alpha(\rho)}{\rho}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}u}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}. Step 3: flipping. The result ( 3.19 ) is close to what we expect, since we retract the spatial average under the Kawasaki dynamics with a weight of particle numbers. The only issue is that the factor \alpha(\rho) behaves badly when \rho is close to 1 . However, as we pointed out, this lifting procedure [u] breaks the symmetry \left\langle{u(\eta)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{u(1-\eta)}\right\rangle_{1-\rho} , where (1-\eta)_{x}=1-\eta_{x} . Therefore, we actually have another version by flipping \rho and 1-\rho . We define \check{u}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} as \check{u}(\eta)=u(1-\eta) and apply ( 3.19 ) to it under \left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{1-\rho} to obtain \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}u^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left\langle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}\check{u}^{2}}\right\rangle_{1-\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\leqslant \displaystyle C\left(\frac{-\log\rho}{1-\rho}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}\check{u}}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{1-\rho}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}. Let us work on a single term. \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\mathbf{N}_{z,n}(\eta)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}\check{u}}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},\mathbf{N}_{z,n}(\eta)}\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{1-\rho} \displaystyle= \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\mathbf{N}_{z,n}(1-\eta)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}\check{u}}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},\mathbf{N}_{z,n}(1-\eta),(1-\eta)\mathbin{\vrule height=4.2194pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.39182pt\vrule height=0.39182pt,depth=0.0pt,width=3.01389pt}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c}}\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle= \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2\left(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|-\mathbf{N}_{z,n}(\eta)\right)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}u}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},\mathbf{N}_{z,n}(\eta),\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=4.2194pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.39182pt\vrule height=0.39182pt,depth=0.0pt,width=3.01389pt}({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+})^{c}}\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. Here from the first line to the second line, we use the observation that \eta has the law \mathbb{P}_{\rho} if and only if 1-\eta has the law \mathbb{P}_{1-\rho} . From the second line to the third line, we use the similar observation that \eta has the law \mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N,\zeta} if and only if 1-\eta has the law \mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,|\Lambda|-N,1-\zeta} . This gives us a flipped version of previous result: (3.20) \left\langle{\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}|}u^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\ \leqslant C\left(\frac{-\log\rho}{1-\rho}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{m}3^{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\left\langle{\frac{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|}{2(|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}|-\mathbf{N}_{z,n})}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\left\langle{\nabla_{x}u}\right\rangle_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+},\mathbf{N}_{z,n}}\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}. We combine the two results, and observe that the factor \frac{-\log(1-\rho)}{\rho} is bounded on [0,\frac{1}{2}] , while \frac{-\log\rho}{1-\rho} is bounded on [\frac{1}{2},1] , we can combine the two estimates into a single one and drop the dependence on \rho . We use ( 3.19 ) for \rho\leqslant\frac{1}{2} and ( 3.20 ) for \rho>\frac{1}{2} , and also simplify the notation using the conditional expectation with respect to \mathcal{G}_{z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}^{+}} . This proves ( 3.15 ). ∎ 4. Subadditive quantities In this section, we start the renormalization approach. We define at first several subadditive quantities and develop their elementary properties. 4.1. Subadditive quantities \overline{\nu} and \overline{\nu}_{*} For every finite set \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}} , we define the quantities (4.1) \begin{split}\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p)&:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\left\{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right\},\\ \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q)&:=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\left\{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}\ell_{q,\Lambda^{+}})(\pi_{b}v)-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right\}.\end{split} Recall that the affine function \ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}} defined in ( 1.51 ), and \Lambda^{-} , \overline{\Lambda^{*}} defined respectively in ( 1.40 ), ( 1.41 ). Compared to ( 1.25 ), here we add the factor \chi(\rho) in the normalization, which will make the notation lighter in the homogenization step. In the following context, we also use \ell_{p} defined in ( 1.49 ) instead of \ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}} to simplify the notation. This formal notation has well-defined gradients and one should consider it as \ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}} when talking about the function itself. We record some elementary properties satisfied by \overline{\nu} and \overline{\nu}_{*} . Proposition 4.1 (Elementary properties of \overline{\nu} and \overline{\nu}_{*} ) . The following properties hold for every bounded \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}} . (1) There exists a unique solution for the optimization problem of \overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p) satisfying \left\langle{v-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=0 ; we denote it by v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) . For the optimization problem of \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda.q) , there exists a unique maximizer u(\cdot,\Lambda,q) being independent of \rho and belonging to \mathcal{F}_{0}(N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+})) satisfying \mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u|\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}]=0 , where \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}} is defined in ( 3.14 ) and N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+}) is defined as N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+}):=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\dist(x,\Lambda^{+})\leqslant\mathbf{r}\}. Moreover, the two optimizers are both harmonic in the sense v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p),u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) , where \mathcal{A}(\Lambda) is defined in ( 1.47 ). (2) There exist two d\times d positive symmetric matrices {\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda) and {\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda) such that for every p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{d} (4.2) \displaystyle\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p)=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p,\qquad\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q)=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda)q and these matrices satisfy \mathsf{Id}\leqslant{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda),{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)\leqslant\lambda\mathsf{Id} . Moreover, for ever p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}} , we have (4.3) \begin{split}p^{\prime}\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p&=\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}\ell_{p^{\prime}})(\pi_{b}v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p))}\right\rangle_{\rho},\\ q^{\prime}\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda)q&=\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}(\pi_{b}\ell_{q^{\prime}})(\pi_{b}u(\cdot,\Lambda,q))}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} (3) For every v^{\prime}\in\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) , we have (4.4) \left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(v-v^{\prime}))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v^{\prime})^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}-\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p), where v=v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) is the minimizer defined in (1). Similarly, for every u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{0} and the maximizer u=u(\cdot,\Lambda,q) defined in (1), we have (4.5) \left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(u-u^{\prime}))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ =\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q)-\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u^{\prime})^{2}+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}u^{\prime})\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. (4) For any partition of vertices \Lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}\Lambda_{i} , we have (4.6) \displaystyle\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p) \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{|\Lambda_{i}|}{|\Lambda|}\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda_{i},p), (4.7) \displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,p) \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{|\Lambda_{i}|}{|\Lambda|}\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda_{i},p). In particular, we have \overline{\nu}(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n+1},q)\leqslant\overline{\nu}(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},q) and \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n+1},q)\leqslant\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},q) for every n\in\mathbb{N}_{+} . Proof. The proof of this proposition is elementary and standard. We give the complete proof of (1), which concerns some details. For other statements, we sketch the main idea of the proof. Readers may look for details in a similar setting in [ 38 , Proposition 4.1] . (1) We first study \overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p) . By a variational calculus, the minimizer can be characterized by the equation that for any \phi\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) , (4.8) \left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(v-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}))(\pi_{b}\phi)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}))(\pi_{b}\phi)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. We can define its solution in the space V=\{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}):\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\rho}=0\}. The coercivity is ensured by Poincaré inequality ( 2.5 ) and we can apply Lax–Milgram theorem to get the unique minimizer v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) . Moreover, ( 4.8 ) implies that v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p)\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) . Then we turn to \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q) . A first observation is that the maximizer can be found in \mathcal{F}_{0}(N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+})) . Because for any u\in\mathcal{F}_{0} , its conditional expectation {\mathsf{A}}_{N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+})}u (defined in ( 2.9 )) reaches a larger value for the functional. More precisely, (4.9) \displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+})}u)^{2}+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+})}u\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &=\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}({\mathsf{A}}_{N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+})}\pi_{b}u)^{2}+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{N_{\mathbf{r}}(\Lambda^{+})}u\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &\geqslant\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)^{2}+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}u)\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho},\end{split} where we use the locality of c_{b} and ( 2.11 ) from the first line to the second line, and Jensen’s inequality from the second line to the third line. Similar to the discussion above, the maximizer of the functional can be characterized by the variational equation that for any \phi\in\mathcal{F}_{0} , (4.10) \displaystyle\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)(\pi_{b}\phi)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}\phi)}\right\rangle_{\rho} Notice that for any \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}} measurable function \phi^{\prime} , the difference \pi_{b}\phi^{\prime} becomes zero and thus the above equation automatically holds. By replacing \phi by \phi-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\phi|\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}] we may only solve the problem for all \phi in the space W=\{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}:\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[f|\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}]=0\} . Moreover, testing the equation with \phi\mathbf{1}_{\{\sum_{x\in\Lambda^{+}}\eta_{x}=N\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta_{y}=\varepsilon_{y},\text{ for finitely many }y\in(\Lambda^{+})^{c}\}} for arbitrary N\in\mathbb{N} and \varepsilon_{y}\in\{0,1\} , we actually reinforce the equation ( 4.10 ) in a stronger way (4.11) \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)(\pi_{b}\phi)\ |\ \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}\phi)\ |\ \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}\right], and we may seek for the solution in the space W . In this space the Poincaré inequality ( 2.8 ) ensures the coercivity, so Lax–Milgram theorem applies and we get the unique maximizer u(\cdot,\Lambda,q) . We emphasize that after taking conditional expectation with respect to proper \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}} , the equation ( 4.11 ) is independent of the choice of \rho . Thus, the optimizer is found separately for every conditional expectation \mathbb{E}[\cdot|\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}] and in particular, u is an optimizer for all \rho . Moreover, testing ( 4.10 ) with \phi\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) , the right hand side becomes zero thanks to ( 3.9 ) and we get u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) . (2) We test ( 4.8 ) with v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p^{\prime})-\ell_{p^{\prime},\Lambda^{+}} and get (4.12) \left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p))(\pi_{b}v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p^{\prime}))}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p))(\pi_{b}\ell_{p^{\prime},\Lambda^{+}})}\right\rangle_{\rho}. In particular, the above formula is linear in p^{\prime} . For the same reason, it is also linear in p . In particular, \overline{\nu} is quadratic in p . To obtain the bound of {\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda) , we use the condition 1\leqslant c_{b}\leqslant\lambda in Hypothesis 1.1 , \begin{split}&\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\ &\leqslant\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)\cdot p\\ &\leqslant\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\lambda(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split} We can observe that \ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}} is the minimizer for \inf_{v\in\ell_{p,\Lambda}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} , whose energy is precisely |p|^{2} . One may see this from the variational characterization ( 4.8 ). This proves the first part of the proposition. The similar argument works for \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q) . Testing ( 4.10 ) with u(\cdot,\Lambda,q^{\prime}) yields the linearity. Concerning the bound for {\overbracket{D}}_{*} , we use the bound for c_{b} to obtain \displaystyle\sup_{v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\left\langle{-\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}+\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}(\pi_{b}\ell_{q,\Lambda^{+}})(\pi_{b}v)}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle\leqslant\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q) \displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\left\langle{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}+\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}(\pi_{b}\ell_{q,\Lambda^{+}})(\pi_{b}v)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. One can see in the lower bound, the maximizer is \ell_{\frac{q}{\lambda}.\Lambda^{+}} , while in the upper bound, the maximizer is \ell_{q,\Lambda^{+}} . This gives the bound we want. (3) This is a direct calculation. We test ( 4.8 ) with (v^{\prime}-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}) to get the first equation, and test the equation ( 4.10 ) with u^{\prime} to get the second equation. (4) For the quantity \overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p) , v^{\prime}=\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda_{i},p)-\ell_{p,\Lambda_{i}^{+}}) is a sub-minimizer of \overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p) , and we use it to prove the sub-additivity of \overline{\nu} . Concerning the quantity \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q) , we can not “glue” the local optimizers, but we use the fact that u(\cdot,\Lambda,q) is a sub-maximizer for every \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda_{i},q) to get the sub-additivity of \overline{\nu}_{*} . We also highlight the identity ( 1.43 ), which helps avoid the boundary layer and is the motivation we use \overline{{\Lambda}^{*}} in the definition ( 4.1 ). ∎ Remark 4.2 . Intuitively, the last part of the proof of item (1) actually says that the maximizer u(\cdot,\Lambda,q) can be found in the following way: first, fix the number of particles inside \Lambda^{+} (denoted by n ) and the environment outside \Lambda^{+} (denoted by \zeta ), and seek for a maximizer u_{n,\zeta} in this condition. Then the maximizer is exactly obtained by pasting all the u_{n,\zeta} ’s. Viewing the sub-additivity ( 4.6 ), we have the following corollary. Corollary 4.3 . For every \rho\in(0,1) , the following limit is well-defined (4.13) \displaystyle{\overbracket{D}}(\rho):=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}). Recall the L^{\infty} norm over \mathcal{X} defined as \left\|{F}\right\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}}|F(\eta)| . Here we give an upper bound estimate of v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) and u(\cdot,\Lambda,q) defined in Proposition 4.1 . Lemma 4.4 ( L^{\infty} estimate) . There exists a constant C(\lambda,d) such that for any connected domain \Lambda of diameter L and p,q\in B_{1}:=\{p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}},|p|=1\} , the following estimate is valid: (4.14) \displaystyle\left\|{v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p)}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|{u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant CL^{d+2}\log L. The proof relies on the mixing time of our non-gradient dynamic; see Appendix B . Note that for 1\leqslant s<\infty , we have a better estimate that \displaystyle\left\|{v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p)}\right\|_{s}+\left\|{u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)}\right\|_{s}\leqslant CL^{d+2}. See Remark B.3 for details. 4.2. Master quantities J We continue to explore the dual property between \overline{\nu} and \overline{\nu}_{*} in this subsection. For every bonded domain \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}} , we define the quantities (4.15) J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q):=\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p)+\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q)-p\cdot q. We first describe J with a variational formula. Lemma 4.5 . (1) For each p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , we have the variational representation (4.16) \displaystyle J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q) \displaystyle:=\sup_{w\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)}J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q;w), where J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q;w) is a functional defined as (4.17) J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q;w)\\ :=\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}w)^{2}-c_{b}(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})(\pi_{b}w)+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}w)\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. One maximizer is (u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)-v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p)) with v,u defined in (1) of Proposition 4.1 . (2) The master quantity J is always positive, i.e. J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)\geqslant 0 . Proof. (1) In the following paragraph, we use v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) to denote the minimizer in the definition of \overline{\nu} and write v=v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) for short. Since we have deduced that the maximizer of \overline{\nu}_{*} can be found in \mathcal{A}(\Lambda) in (1) of Proposition 4.1 , we may write \displaystyle J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)= \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle+\sup_{u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)^{2}+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}u)\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle-p\cdot q. Since (v-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}})\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) , we have (4.18) \left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}(\pi_{b}(v-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}))(\pi_{b}\ell_{q,\Lambda^{+}})}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{q\cdot\nabla_{x}(v-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}})}\right\rangle_{\rho}=0. Here the first equality comes from ( 1.52 ), ( 1.54 ), ( 1.55 ) and the second equality comes from ( 3.9 ). Moreover, for any u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) , by its definition ( 1.47 ) and noting (v-\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}})\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}) again, we have \left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)(\pi_{b}v)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)(\pi_{b}\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}})}\right\rangle_{\rho}, and in particular this equation is true when taking u=v . Combining these results, we obtain \displaystyle J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q) \displaystyle=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)}\left(\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)^{2}+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}u)\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right. \displaystyle\quad-\left.\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right) \displaystyle=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(u-v))^{2}-c_{b}(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})\pi_{b}(u-v)+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})\pi_{b}(u-v)\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=\sup_{w\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}w)^{2}-c_{b}(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})(\pi_{b}w)+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}w)\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho}. From the definition of u(\cdot,\Lambda,q) , we conclude that w=u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)-v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) is exactly a maximizer. (2) We test the functional in the definition of \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q) with the minimizer v=v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p) of \overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p) and obtain \displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q) \displaystyle\geqslant\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}v)\right)}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=-\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p)+\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})}\right\rangle_{\rho} \displaystyle=-\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p)+p\cdot q, which proves J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)\geqslant 0 . Here from the first line to the second line, we also use the identity ( 4.18 ). ∎ Now we explain why J is convenient in estimating the convergence rate. Lemma 4.6 . (1) For any bounded \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and \rho\in(0,1) , we have {\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)\geqslant{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda) . (2) There exists a constant C(d,\lambda) such that for every symmetric matrix \widetilde{D} , we have (4.19) |\widetilde{D}-{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)|+|\widetilde{D}-{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)|\leqslant C\sup_{|p|=1}J(\rho,\Lambda,p,\widetilde{D}p)^{\frac{1}{2}}. Proof. (1) Recall J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)\geqslant 0 from (2) of Lemma 4.5 , and insert q={\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)p to obtain \displaystyle 0\leqslant \displaystyle J(\rho,\Lambda,p,{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)p) \displaystyle= \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p+\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)p-p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)p \displaystyle= \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p-\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)p, so we have {\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)\geqslant{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda) . (2) Using the property {\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)\geqslant{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda) , we have \displaystyle J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)= \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p+\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda)q-p\cdot q \displaystyle\geqslant \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p+\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda)q-p\cdot q \displaystyle= \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}({\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p-q)\cdot{\overbracket{D}}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda)({\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)p-q). Setting |p|=1 and q=\widetilde{D}p , we conclude that |{\overbracket{D}}(\rho,\Lambda)-\widetilde{D}|\leqslant C\sup_{|p|=1}J(\rho,\Lambda,p,\widetilde{D}p)^{\frac{1}{2}}. The proof of the statement concerning |{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda)-\widetilde{D}| is similar. ∎ This lemma allows us to control the convergence rate of {\overbracket{D}}(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}) by showing the convergence rate of J(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m},p,{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})p) . Finally, we summarize some more properties of J similar to the properties for \overline{\nu} and \overline{\nu}_{*} . Proposition 4.7 (Elementary properties of J ) . For every \rho\in(0,1) , every bounded \Lambda\subsetneq\mathbb{Z}^{d} and every p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , the quantity J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q) defined as ( 4.15 ) satisfies the following properties: (1) First order variation and optimizer: the optimization problem in ( 4.16 ) admits a unique solution v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q)\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) such that \mathbb{E}_{\rho}[v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda.p,q)|\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}]=0 , which can be expressed in terms of the optimizers of \overline{\nu} and \overline{\nu}_{*} as (4.20) v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q)=u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)-v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,q)-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)-v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p)|\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda^{+}}]. This solution v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q) satisfies that for every w\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) , (4.21) \left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}(c_{b}\pi_{b}v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q))(\pi_{b}w)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}(-c_{b}(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})(\pi_{b}w)+(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}w))}\right\rangle_{\rho}, and the mapping (p,q)\mapsto v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q) is linear. (2) Quadratic response: we have a quadratic expression for J (4.22) J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)=\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}. For every w\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda) , with the functional defined in ( 4.17 ), we have (4.23) \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(w-v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q)))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)-J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q;w). (3) For any partition of vertices \Lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}\Lambda_{i} , we have (4.24) J(\rho,\Lambda,p,q)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{|\Lambda_{i}|}{|\Lambda|}J(\rho,\Lambda_{i},p,q). (4) Slope property: with the gradient operator \nabla_{x} defined in ( 1.54 ), the optimizer v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q) satisfies the slop property (4.25) \displaystyle\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\nabla_{x}v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q)}\right\rangle_{\rho}={\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda)q-p. Proof. (1) The equation ( 4.21 ) comes directly from the first order variation calculus. The proof of existence and uniqueness of the solution v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q) is similar to the one for \overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda,q) . One can check directly that v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p_{1},q_{1})+v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p_{2},q_{2}) is the solution for the problem ( 4.21 ) with parameter (p_{1}+p_{2},q_{1}+q_{2}) and it also satisfies the conditional expectation condition, so we have v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p_{1}+p_{2},q_{1}+q_{2})=v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p_{1},q_{1})+v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p_{2},q_{2}) by the uniqueness, which implies that the mapping (p,q)\mapsto v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p,q) is linear. The exact expression ( 4.20 ) follows directly from the fact that \left(u(\cdot,\Lambda,q)-v(\cdot,\rho,\Lambda,p)\right) is a maximizer in ( 4.16 ), and we add the necessary regularization condition.
