In ( 7.39 ), r(F_{N})^{d} needs to be (r(F_{N})+\mathbf{r}+1)^{d} , but this difference is absorbed to the constant C by changing it. ∎ 7.3.3. \Omega_{1} by the gradient replacement This part is a highlight of the non-gradient and quantitative method. The term \Omega_{1} in Lemma 7.6 looks like of the order O(N) . We need the gradient replacement to eliminate the diverging factor in this term, with proper error estimates; see Proposition 7.10 and Lemma 7.12 . The main inputs comes from Section 6.6 , where we state the decay estimates for the CLT variances. In particular, Propositions 6.11 and 7.1 are new and essential to show the quantitative result. The following proposition is a refinement of Theorem 3.2 of [ 36 ] . We recall that A_{L}=A_{L}(\xi) is the \mathbb{R}^{d} -valued function defined by ( 6.78 ), and ( 1.20 ) for R(\rho;F)=\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)-\mathbf{c}(\rho) , and ( 1.15 ) and ( 1.14 ) for \mathbf{c}(\rho) and \mathbf{c}(\rho;F) , respectively. Proposition 7.10 (Gradient replacement) . For every T>0 , there exists a positive constant C(d,\mathbf{r},T)<\infty , such that the following estimates hold for all t\in[0,T] and \beta>0 : \displaystyle\int_{0}^{t} \displaystyle E^{f^{N}_{s}}\!\Bigg[\Omega_{1}+N^{1-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\cdot D(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})L^{-d}\tau_{x}A_{L} \displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{\beta dN^{-d}}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\cdot R(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L};F_{N})\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\Bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}s \displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{\beta}+\beta^{2}Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(\lambda,F_{N})+\beta Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F_{N}), and the upper bound for Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(\lambda,F_{N}) (7.40) |Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(\lambda,F_{N})|\leqslant CN^{-1}L^{(2d+4)}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{3}(1+r(F_{N})^{3d}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty}^{3}), and the domination for Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F_{N}) (7.41) |Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F_{N})|\leqslant dT\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}Q_{L}(F_{N}). Here the upper bound Q_{L}(F_{N}) appears in the computation of the CLT variance in Proposition 6.10 . To prove Proposition 7.10 , the first step is to reduce a non-equilibrium problem into a static problem under the canonical equilibrium measure, so we can make use of the results in Section 6.6 . We need the following key lemma, which is sometimes called Kipnis–Varadhan estimate or Itô–Tanaka trick. It is a result similar to Lemmas 3.3 (the case {\mathbf{U}}(\rho)=0 ) and 6.1 of [ 36 ] , but providing a fine error estimate. Recall the notation \mathcal{X}_{\Lambda},\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda,M},\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,M,\zeta} and \langle\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\Lambda,M,\zeta} introduced at the beginning of Section 6.6 , and \mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta} as the generator defined by ( 6.77 ). The following lemma will be used for the proof of Proposition 7.10 taking n=d and for the proof of Lemma 7.12 taking n=1 . Lemma 7.11 . Let n\in\mathbb{N} , J(t,v)=\{J_{i}(t,v)\}_{i=1}^{n}\in C^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{n}) , and {\mathbf{U}}(\rho)=\{{\mathbf{U}}_{ij}(\rho)\}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}\in C([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}) , and G(\eta)=\{G_{i}(\eta)\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{n} be functions satisfying \displaystyle\forall\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}},0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|,\qquad\langle G\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}=0. There exists a positive constant C(d,\mathbf{r},T)<\infty such that for every t\in[0,T] and \beta>0 , we have an estimate \displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}E^{f_{s}^{N}}\bigg[N^{1-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}J(s,x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}G(\eta)-d\beta N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}J(s,x/N)\cdot{\mathbf{U}}(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})J(s,x/N)\bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}s \displaystyle\leqslant d\beta\,t\sup_{|q|\leqslant\|J\|_{\infty}}\sup_{0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|,\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}}}\Big(\,|\Lambda_{L}|\big\langle(q\cdot G)\,(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}(q\cdot G),\big\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}-q\cdot{\mathbf{U}}(M/|\Lambda_{L}|)q\Big) \displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{\beta}+\beta^{2}Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(J,G). The error term Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(J,G) has a bound (7.42) \displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(J,G)|\leqslant CN^{-1}L^{(2d+4)}\|G\|_{\infty}^{3}\|J\|_{\infty}^{3}. Lemma 7.11 is a combination of essentially known results, providing fine error estimates, so the proof is postponed to Appendix C . Proof of Proposition 7.10 . To rewrite the function in the expectation in Proposition 7.10 , we recall three \mathbb{R}^{d} -valued functions A_{L}(\xi) , B_{L,\zeta}(\xi) , and H_{L,\zeta,F}(\xi) of \eta=\xi\cdot\zeta introduced in ( 6.78 ) and \xi=\eta|_{\Lambda_{L}} and \zeta=\eta|_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}} . We now apply Lemma 7.11 taking n=d and (7.43) \displaystyle\begin{aligned} J(t,v)&=\nabla\lambda(t,v),\\ G(\eta)&=L^{-d}\left\{D(\overline{\eta}_{L})A_{L}(\xi)-B_{L,\zeta}(\xi)+H_{L,\zeta,F_{N}}(\xi)\right\},\\ {\mathbf{U}}(\rho)&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{c}(\rho;F_{N})-\mathbf{c}(\rho)\right)=\frac{1}{2}R(\rho;F_{N}).\end{aligned} Note that, under the above choice, the expressions under \int_{0}^{t}E^{f^{N}_{s}}[\cdots]{\mathrm{d}}s in Proposition 7.10 and Lemma 7.11 coincide, by recalling that \Omega_{1} is defined by ( 7.26 ) and ( 7.28 ). Then, we have \|G\|_{\infty}\leqslant C(1+r(F_{N})^{d}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty}) . Indeed, the function D=D(\rho) is bounded (recall ( 1.17 )), \|A_{L}\|_{\infty}\leqslant CL^{d} , \|B_{L,\zeta}\|_{\infty}\leqslant CL^{d} since W_{x,y} is bounded, and from \displaystyle H_{L,\zeta,F_{N}}=\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\Lambda_{L}^{*}}c_{xy}(\xi\cdot\zeta)\pi_{xy}\Big(\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{L-r(F_{N})-1}}\tau_{z}F_{N}\Big), we have \displaystyle\|H_{L,\zeta,F_{N}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant CL^{d}r(F_{N})^{d}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty}. Therefore, by ( 7.42 ), the error Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(J,G) with J and G determined as above has the estimate ( 7.40 ). Thus, the proof of Proposition 7.10 is concluded with the help of Proposition 6.10 , by observing that (7.44) Q_{L}^{(2)}(\|J\|_{\infty},F_{N})\\ :=dT\sup_{|q|\leqslant\|J\|_{\infty}}\sup_{0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|,\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}}}\Big\{L^{d}\langle(q\cdot G),(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}(q\cdot G)\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}-q\cdot{\mathbf{U}}(M/L^{d})q\Big\} appearing from Lemma 7.11 is bounded by dT\|J\|_{\infty}^{2}Q_{L}(F_{N}) with Q_{L}(F_{N}) given in Proposition 6.10 . Taking J=\nabla\lambda and denoting Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F_{N}):=\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}Q_{L}(F_{N}) , we obtain ( 7.41 ) and conclude the proof of Proposition 7.10 . ∎ In the first displayed formula in Proposition 7.10 , the term next to \Omega_{1} still looks like diverging order O(N) from the front factor N^{1-d} . In the following lemma, we evaluate it and reduce it into another term of order O(1) . Lemma 7.12 . Set \displaystyle Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda):=\int_{0}^{t}E^{f^{N}_{s}} \displaystyle\bigg[N^{1-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\cdot D(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})L^{-d}\tau_{x}A_{L}(\eta) \displaystyle\qquad+N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda(s,x/N)P_{ij}(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})\bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}s, where we have (7.45) \displaystyle\rho\in[0,1],\quad P(\rho)\equiv\{P_{ij}(\rho)\}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}:=\int_{0}^{\rho}D(\rho^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho^{\prime}. There exists a positive constant C(d,\mathbf{r},T)<\infty such that for every t\in[0,T] and \beta>0 the following upper bound holds for Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda) : \displaystyle Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda)\leqslant C(L^{-1}\|\nabla^{2}\lambda\|_{\infty}+N^{-1}\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty})+C\beta L^{-1}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{C}{\beta}+C\beta^{2}N^{-1}L^{2d-2}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{3}. It is a refined version of [ 36 , Lemma 3.4] . The outline, following that of [ 34 , Lemma 4.3] , is given in Appendix C by applying Lemma 7.11 . 7.3.4. Large deviation estimate Here, we have finished the step of the one-block estimate and the gradient replacement, so let us summarize the outcome of Lemma 7.6 , Corollary 7.9 and Proposition 7.10 together with Lemma 7.12 in the form of the following Lemma 7.13 . We recall h_{N}(t) defined in ( 7.6 ) and R(\rho;F) defined in ( 1.20 ), and the matrix P(\rho) defined in ( 7.45 ). Lemma 7.13 . Assume \lambda\in C^{1,3}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}) , \rho(t,v)=\overline{\rho}(\lambda(t,v)) as defined in ( 7.8 ), F_{N}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} satisfying the conditions ( 7.30 ), ( 7.34 ) and ( 7.38 ), then there exists a finite constant C(d,\mathbf{r},\rho,\lambda,T)<\infty , such that the following estimate holds for every \beta>0 (7.46) h_{N}(t)\leqslant h_{N}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}E^{f^{N}_{s}}[W_{s}]\,{\mathrm{d}}s+C(\beta+1)\,\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F_{N})|\\ +\int_{0}^{t}\left(Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}s. On the right-hand side of ( 7.46 ), the function W_{t} is defined by \displaystyle W_{t}(\eta)= \displaystyle-N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\dot{\lambda}(t,x/N)\{\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L}-\rho(t,x/N)\} \displaystyle+N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\operatorname{Tr}\left(\nabla^{2}\lambda(t,x/N)\{P(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})-P(\rho(t,x/N))\}\right) \displaystyle+\frac{N^{-d}}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(t,x/N)\cdot\{\mathbf{c}(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})-\mathbf{c}(\rho(t,x/N))\}\nabla\lambda(t,x/N). The term Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N}) is as in Lemma 7.6 , and the term Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N}) has a decomposition as (7.47) \displaystyle Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N}):=\frac{C}{\beta}+\beta^{2}Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(\lambda,F_{N})+\beta Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda). See Proposition 7.10 for Q_{N,L}^{(1)} , Q_{L}^{(2)} and Lemma 7.12 for Q_{N,L}^{(3)} . The error Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N}) is estimated as (7.48) \displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})| \displaystyle\leqslant C\big(N^{-1}L^{(d+2)/2}+L^{-d}(1+r(F_{N})^{d})\big) \displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\|\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}(1+r(F_{N})^{d}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty})^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\lambda\|_{\infty}(1+r(F_{N})^{d+1}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty})\Big) \displaystyle\;\;+CN^{-1}(\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla^{2}\lambda\|_{\infty}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}). Proof. The proof is similar to [ 36 , Lemma 3.2] . First, we integrate the estimate for \partial_{t}h_{N}(t) shown in Lemma 7.6 on the time integral [0,t] . Then, for \Omega_{1} , we apply Proposition 7.10 (with an error \frac{C}{\beta}+\beta^{2}Q_{N,L}^{(1)}+\beta Q_{L}^{(2)} ) and Lemma 7.12 (with an error Q_{N,L}^{(3)} ), and obtain a replacement of \int_{0}^{t}E^{f^{N}_{s}}[\Omega_{1}]\,{\mathrm{d}}s by the following integral: \displaystyle\int_{0}^{t} \displaystyle\bigg\{E^{f^{N}_{s}}\bigg[N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\operatorname{Tr}\left(\nabla^{2}\lambda(s,x/N)P(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})\right)\bigg] \displaystyle\quad+E^{f^{N}_{s}}\bigg[\frac{\beta N^{-d}}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\cdot R(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L};F_{N})\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\bigg]\bigg\}\,{\mathrm{d}}s, with an error Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N}) defined in ( 7.47 ). The second term in the above formula is bounded by C\beta\,\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sup_{\rho}|R(\rho;F_{N})| and this is counted on the right-hand side of ( 7.46 ) for h_{N}(t) . For \Omega_{2} , by Corollary 7.9 , \int_{0}^{t}E^{f^{N}_{s}}[\Omega_{2}]\,{\mathrm{d}}s can be replaced by the expression in ( 7.37 ): \displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}E^{f^{N}_{s}} \displaystyle\bigg[N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\Big\{-\dot{\lambda}(s,x/N)\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L} \displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{x\in\Gamma_{N}}}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\cdot\mathbf{c}(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L};F_{N})\nabla\lambda(s,x/N)\Big\}\bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}s, with the error Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2},1}(\lambda,F_{N}) defined in ( 7.39 ). This gives the first part in the error estimate of |Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})| . Note that \mathbf{c}(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L};F_{N}) in the above expression (i.e. ( 7.37 )) can be replaced by \mathbf{c}(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L}) with an error C\,\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sup_{\rho}|R(\rho;F_{N})| . The reason that we have the second part in the error estimate of |Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})| in ( 7.48 ) is as follows. Recalling that \rho(t,v)=\overline{\rho}(\lambda(t,v)) , we have the identity (7.49) \displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\operatorname{Tr}\big(\nabla^{2}\lambda(t,v)P(\rho(t,v))\big){\mathrm{d}}v=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(t,v)\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho(t,v))\nabla\lambda(t,v){\mathrm{d}}v. This is shown by integration by parts and noting LHS of ( 7.49 ) \displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}-(\partial_{v_{i}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda)(t,v)P_{i,j}(\rho(t,v))\,{\mathrm{d}}v \displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda(t,v)\partial_{v_{i}}P_{i,j}(\rho(t,v))\,{\mathrm{d}}v \displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda(t,v)\partial_{\rho}P_{i,j}(\rho(t,v))\partial_{\lambda}\rho(t,v)\partial_{v_{i}}\lambda(t,v)\,{\mathrm{d}}v \displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda(t,v)D_{i,j}(\rho(t,v))\chi(\rho(t,v))\partial_{v_{i}}\lambda(t,v)\,{\mathrm{d}}v \displaystyle=\text{RHS of \eqref{3.IBP}}. The third line makes use of the chain rule. The fourth line makes use of \frac{\partial\overline{\rho}}{\partial\lambda}=\chi(\rho) in ( 7.8 ), the definition of P(\rho(t,v)) in ( 7.45 ). The last relies on the Einstein relation ( 1.12 ). The terms with \rho(t,x/N) in the second and third terms of W cancels by this identity with an error bounded by \displaystyle CN^{-1}(\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla^{2}\lambda\|_{\infty}\|\nabla\rho\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla^{2}\lambda\|_{\infty}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\|\nabla\rho\|_{\infty}), which appears when we discretize the above two integrals in ( 7.49 ); note that P,\mathbf{c} are bounded and P,\mathbf{c}\in C^{\infty}((0,1)) as proved in [ 16 ] . ∎ We aim to treat the term E^{f^{N}_{t}}[W_{t}] in Lemma 7.13 via a large deviation estimate. Using the entropy inequality, it can be reduced to the expectation under {P^{\psi^{N}_{t}}=\psi^{N}_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N}} : (7.50) \forall\delta>0,\qquad E^{f^{N}_{t}}[W_{t}]\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta N^{d}}\log E^{\psi^{N}_{t}}\left[e^{\delta N^{d}W_{t}}\right]+\frac{1}{\delta}h_{N}(t). We study the asymptotic behavior of the first term on the right-hand side via a large deviation type upper bound with an appropriate error estimate under \psi^{N}_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N} ; see Lemma 7.14 below. For \lambda(\cdot)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}) and F=F(\eta)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} , dropping the t -dependence in ( 7.5 ), the local equilibrium state \psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}(\eta){\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N} of second order approximation is a probability measure on \mathcal{X}_{N} defined by (7.51) \psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}(\eta)=Z^{-1}\exp\bigg\{\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\lambda(x/N)\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}F(\eta)\bigg\}, for \eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N} , where Z=Z_{\lambda(\cdot),F,N} is the normalization constant with respect to \nu^{N} . Then we have the following large deviation type upper bound for \psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N} with the error estimate. Recall that \nu^{N}_{\rho} is the the product Bernoulli measure of parameter \rho on {\mathcal{X}_{N}=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}} . We define that \displaystyle\overline{\nu}^{N}_{\lambda}:=\nu^{N}_{\rho(\lambda)}, with \rho=\overline{\rho}(\lambda) defined as in ( 7.8 ). Then the rate function for the large deviation principle for the Bernoulli measure \overline{\nu}^{N}_{\lambda} is denoted by I(u;\lambda) , namely, for u\in[0,1] , (7.52) \displaystyle\begin{aligned} &I(u;\lambda):=-\lambda u-q(u)+p(\lambda),\\ &p(\lambda):=\log(e^{\lambda}+1),\quad q(u):=-\{u\log u+(1-u)\log(1-u)\}.\end{aligned} Lemma 7.14 (Appendix B of [ 34 ] ) . For every G(v,u)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,1]) , we define its sum with respect to the local density (7.53) \widetilde{G}_{N,L}(\eta):=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}G(x/N,\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L}). The following large deviation estimate then holds \displaystyle N^{-d}\log E^{\psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}}\left[\exp\widetilde{G}_{N,L}(\eta)\right] \displaystyle\leqslant \displaystyle\sup_{u(v)\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d};[0,1])}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\{G(v,u(v))-I(u(v);\lambda(v))\}\,{\mathrm{d}}v+Q_{N,L}^{LD}(\lambda,F;G). The error term Q_{N,L}^{LD}(\lambda,F;G) has an estimate: \displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{LD}(\lambda,F;G)|\leqslant \displaystyle CN^{-1}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}+CN^{-1}L\Big(\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{v}G\|_{\infty}\Big) \displaystyle+CL^{-d}\Big(\log L+\|\lambda\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{u}G\|_{\infty}\Big), where \partial_{v}G and \partial_{u}G are respectively the derivatives with respect to its first and second variable. Proof. It suffices to take G_{1}=0,G_{2}=G and K=1 in Appendix B of [ 34 ] . ∎ We then apply the large deviation estimate to h_{N}(t) . Recall Lemma 7.12 for P(\rho) , and ( 7.52 ) for I(u;\lambda) . We define g_{\delta}(t) as follows: \displaystyle g_{\delta}(t):=\sup_{u(v)\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d};[0,1])}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\{\delta\cdot\sigma(u(v);t,v)-I(u(v);\lambda(t,v))\}\,{\mathrm{d}}v, \displaystyle\sigma(u;t,v):=-\dot{\lambda}(t,v)\{u-\rho(t,v)\}+\operatorname{Tr}\left(\nabla^{2}\lambda(t,v)\{P(u)-P(\rho(t,v))\}\right)\phantom{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\lambda(t,v)\cdot\{\mathbf{c}(u)-\mathbf{c}(\rho(t,v))\}\nabla\lambda(t,v). The following proposition gives the detail of the error terms in Theorem 2.1 of [ 36 ] . Proposition 7.15 . Under the same setting as Lemma 7.13 , there exists a constant {C(d,\mathbf{r},\rho,\lambda,T)<\infty} such that for every \beta>0 and \delta>0 , we have (7.54) h_{N}(t)\leqslant h_{N}(0)+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}g_{\delta}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}h_{N}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\ +C(\beta+1)\,\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F_{N})|+Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N})\\ +\int_{0}^{t}\left(Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}s. Here the term Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N}) is defined using Q_{N,L}^{LD} in Lemma 7.14 \displaystyle Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N}):=\int_{0}^{T}Q_{N,L}^{LD}(\lambda(s),F_{N};\delta\cdot\sigma(u;t,v))\,{\mathrm{d}}s. Proof. The result is immediate by combing Lemma 7.13 , the entropy inequality ( 7.50 ) and Lemma 7.14 . In view of W_{t} given in Lemma 7.13 , we set G in Lemma 7.14 as \displaystyle G(v,u)=\delta\cdot\sigma(u;t,v). ∎ Remark 7.16 . Under the setting \lambda(t,v)=\overline{\lambda}(\rho(t,v)) , there exists \delta_{0}>0 , such that for every \delta\in(0,\delta_{0}) , we have (7.55) \displaystyle\forall t\in[0,T],\qquad g_{\delta}(t)\leqslant 0. This observation ( 7.55 ) was proved in [ 36 , (2.6)] . 7.3.5. Concluding the proof of Proposition 7.3 Proof of Proposition 7.3 . Firstly, we choose the parameters L,N as the setting of Lemma 7.13 and Proposition 7.15 , and assume ( 7.9 ) in Condition 7.2 . The constant C>0 in this proof may change from line to line and may depend on \rho_{0} . Step 1: relative entropy estimate. In the bound h_{N}(t) obtained in Proposition 7.13 , thanks to the observation ( 7.55 ), the term g_{\delta} can be dropped. The Gronwall’s inequality applies and we obtain the estimate (7.56) 0\leqslant h_{N}(t)\leqslant e^{t/\delta}\Big(h_{N}(0)+C(\beta+1)\,\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F_{N})|+Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N})\\ +\int_{0}^{t}\left(Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\Big), for every \beta>0 , t\in[0,T] and 0<\delta<\delta_{0} . We then treat the bounds term by term. Term 1: Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N}) . In ( 7.56 ), we recall ( 7.47 ) that the first error Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N}) for the microscopic current of non-gradient type adjusting with the corrector F_{N} consists of four terms as \displaystyle Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})=\frac{C}{\beta}+\beta^{2}Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(\lambda,F_{N})+\beta Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda). Here, the term Q_{N,L}^{(1)} has a bound (see Proposition 7.10 ) (7.57) \displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(\lambda,F_{N})|\leqslant CN^{-1}L^{2d+4}(1+r(F_{N})^{3d}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty}^{3}). Note that \lambda(t,v) is smooth, in particular, {\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\nabla\lambda(t)\|_{\infty}<\infty} . The term Q_{L}^{(2)} is estimated by ( 7.41 ) as (7.58) \displaystyle|Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F_{N})|\leqslant C(1+r(F_{N})^{2d})(1+r(F_{N})^{2d}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty}^{2})L^{-1}+CL^{-\gamma_{2}}. The term Q_{N,L}^{(3)} does not depend on F_{N} and it is estimated in Lemma 7.12 (7.59) \displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda)|\leqslant C(L^{-1}+N^{-1})+C\beta L^{-1}+\frac{C}{\beta}+C\beta^{2}N^{-1}L^{2d-2}. Term 2: Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N}) . The error Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N}) for the gradient term is bounded as (7.60) \displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})|\leqslant \displaystyle C\big(N^{-1}L^{(d+2)/2}+L^{-d}(1+r(F_{N})^{d})\big) \displaystyle\times(1+r(F_{N})^{2d}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty}^{2}+r(F_{N})^{d+1}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty})+CN^{-1}. This estimate is provided in Lemma 7.13 . Term 3: Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N}) . The error Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N}) appearing in a large deviation bound and is estimated as (7.61) \displaystyle|Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N})|\leqslant CN^{-1}(L+\|F_{N}\|_{\infty})+CL^{-d}\log L. This estimate is shown in Lemma 7.14 . Term 4: Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N}) . The error Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N}) in the entropy calculation, especially in the time derivative of h_{N}(t) , has a bound (7.62) \displaystyle|Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N})|\leqslant CN^{-1}(1+r(F_{N})^{d+2}\|F_{N}\|_{\infty})^{3}, if the condition ( 7.30 ) is satisfied; see Lemma 7.6 . Step 2: choice of parameters. Now, according to Condition 7.2 , we set \displaystyle 1\ll n=n(N)\ll L=L(N)\ll N,\qquad F_{N}=\Phi_{n(N)}, based on Proposition 7.1 . More precisely, we set n,L,\beta as mesoscopic scales (7.63) n:=\lfloor N^{s_{1}}\rfloor<L:=\lfloor N^{s_{2}}\rfloor<N,\quad 0<s_{1}<s_{2}<1,\qquad\beta:=n^{s_{3}},\quad s_{3}>0, with s_{1},s_{2},s_{3} to be determined. Then, from ( 7.56 ) and the estimates ( 7.57 )–( 7.62 ) stated above, we obtain that \displaystyle h_{N}(t)\leqslant C\Big(h_{N}(0) \displaystyle+n^{-s_{3}}+n^{4d+6}N^{-1}L^{2d+4}+n^{s_{3}}L^{-\gamma} \displaystyle+n^{4s+6}L^{-1}+L^{-d}\log L+n^{s_{3}}n^{-\gamma_{2}}\Big), where \gamma,\gamma_{2} are in Theorem 1.3 and Proposition 7.1 . Since h_{N}(0)\leqslant C_{1}N^{-\kappa_{1}} , choosing s_{3}\in(0,\gamma) , s_{2}=\frac{1}{2d+5} and then s_{1}\in(0,\frac{s_{2}}{4d+6}) small enough, one can derive (7.64) \displaystyle h_{N}(t)\leqslant CN^{-\kappa}, for some C,\kappa>0 . Note that the conditions ( 7.30 ), ( 7.34 ) and ( 7.38 ) in Proposition 7.13 are all satisfied under the above choice of s_{1},s_{2} for n,L in ( 7.63 ). ∎ 8. Extension to disordered lattice gas We have worked with the non-gradient model where the jump rate is non-constant but a deterministic function on the configuration space. One may wonder whether the method is effective when the external randomness comes into the jump rate function. These models are known as the disordered lattice gas or the exclusion process in random/inhomogeneous environment . In the literature, [ 74 , 32 ] considered the lattice gas with disorder on site; [ 45 , 51 , 53 ] studied the cases where the jump rate depends on the random environment and combines the homogenization theory to obtain the diffusion matrix; [ 30 , 31 ] further relaxed the underlying graph to the supercritical percolation and other stationary random graphs. It is not immediate to cover the quantitative homogenization results in all these models, because there are various ways to pose the disorder and sometimes the jump rate also degenerates. Here we just give one typical example to illustrate that our proof still works in the presence of external disorder. This model can be seen as a lattice gas with disorder on bonds, where randomness is introduced without breaking the spatial homogeneous property, the uniform ellipticity and the product Bernoulli measure is still an invariant measure. The argument will give a quantitative convergence rate (with a random fluctuation) of finite-volume conductivity. 8.1. Model and hypothesis The notations in this new model is almost the same of the original one. However, instead of the generator ( 1.9 ) defined by a collection of functions \{c_{b}(\eta)\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}} , we now consider a collection of random functions: \displaystyle c:\ \Omega \displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}^{({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}, \displaystyle\omega \displaystyle\mapsto\{c_{b}^{\omega}(\cdot)\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}, on some probability space (\Omega,\mathscr{G},\mathbf{P}) satisfying the following hypothesis. Hypothesis 8.1 . The following conditions are supposed for c:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}^{({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}} . (1) Non-degenerate and local: there exists a positive integer \mathbf{r} and a positive number \lambda>1 such that for every \omega , the function c^{\omega}_{x,y}(\eta) depends only on \{\eta_{z}:|z-x|\leqslant\mathbf{r}\} , and is bounded on two sides 1\leqslant c^{\omega}_{x,y}(\eta)\leqslant\lambda . (2) Detailed balance under Bernoulli measures: for every \omega , the function c^{\omega}_{x,y}(\eta) does not depend on \{\eta_{x},\eta_{y}\} . (3) Spatially homogeneous: the joint distribution of (c_{x,x+e}^{\omega})_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},e\in U} is the same as that of (\tau_{x}c_{0,e}^{\omega})_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},e\in U} , where both of them are viewed as a family of random functions taking value in \mathcal{F}_{0} . (4) Unit range dependence: for any two edge sets E,F\subseteq({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*} such that if there is no adjacent pair b\in E,\ b^{\prime}\in F sharing a common vertex, then the functions \{c_{b}^{\cdot}\}_{b\in E} and \{c_{b}^{\cdot}\}_{b\in F} are independent. We specially mention that given each sample \omega\in\Omega , the functions \{c_{b}^{\omega}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}} satisfy the same properties as that in Hypothesis 1.1 except the spatial homogeneous property, which is replaced by the equality in distribution. Meanwhile, the detailed balance condition ensures that the product Bernoulli measure \operatorname{Ber}(\rho)^{\otimes{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}} is still an invariant measure for the Kawasaki dynamics of jump rate \{c_{b}^{\omega}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}} for every \rho\in[0,1] . 8.2. Quantitative stochastic homogenization We use the renormalization approach to establish the quantitative homogenization result. Given \omega\in\Omega , we define the quenched subadditive quantities \overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p) and \overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q) as in ( 4.1 ) (8.1) \begin{split}\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p)&:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\left\{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}c^{\omega}_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right\},\\ \overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q)&:=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\left\{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}v)-\frac{1}{2}c^{\omega}_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right\}.\end{split} Then for each \omega , (1)-(4) in Proposition 4.1 still hold except ( 4.6 ) and ( 4.7 ). This gives the definition of the quenched diffusion matrix and conductivity \displaystyle\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)p,\qquad\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)=2\chi(\rho){\overbracket{D}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda), \displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)q,\qquad\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)=2\chi(\rho){\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda). The results ( 4.6 ) and ( 4.7 ) are missing, because now for every \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} and z\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} we only have \overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,z+\Lambda,p)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p) (from (3) of Hypothesis 8.1 ) instead of \overline{\nu}(\rho,z+\Lambda,p)=\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p) in Proposition 4.1 . Nevertheless, denoting by \mathbf{E} the expectation associated to (\Omega,\mathscr{G},\mathbf{P}) , we recover (8.2) \begin{split}\mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m+1},p)]&\leqslant\mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m},p)],\\ \mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}_{*}(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m+1},q)]&\leqslant\mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}_{*}(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m},q)].\end{split} The monotone property allows us to define the limit {\overbracket{D}}(\rho):=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{E}[{\overbracket{D}}(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})],\qquad\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho):=2\chi(\rho){\overbracket{D}}(\rho). Our goal is to prove the quantitative convergence rate of the finite-volume quenched conductivity matrix to this limit. Because \overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda) depends on the random environment \omega , we need to measure the random fluctuation and we use the following notation introduced in [ 11 , Appendix A] : for a random variable X in (\Omega,\mathscr{G},\mathbf{P}) and exponents s,\theta\in(0,\infty) , we define (8.3) X\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{s}(\theta)\Longleftrightarrow\mathbf{E}[\exp((\theta^{-1}X_{+})^{s})]\leqslant 2, where X_{+}:=\max\{X,0\} . Roughly, ( 8.3 ) tells that X is concentrated with a stretched exponential tail. More properties of \mathcal{O}_{s} can be found in [ 11 , Appendix A] . Using this notation, we state our result as follows. Theorem 8.2 . Fix t\in(0,d) , there exists \beta(d,\lambda)>0 , C(t,d,\lambda)<\infty such that for any \rho\in(0,1) and any m\in\mathbb{N}_{+} : (8.4) |\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)-\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})|+|\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})|\leqslant\left(C3^{-m\beta(d-t)}+\mathcal{O}_{1}(C3^{-mt})\right). Proof. We follow [ 11 , Theorem 2.4] to handle the fluctuation part. We combine the quenched subadditive quantity defined in ( 8.1 ) to obtain the quenched master quantity (8.5) \displaystyle J(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p,q):=\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p)+\overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q)-p\cdot q. Then for each \omega fixed, Lemmas 4.5 and 4.6 remain valid and especially we have \displaystyle\left|\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})\right|\leqslant C(d,\lambda)\chi(\rho)\left(\sup_{|p|=1}J(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right). One can verify that J(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p,q) is a subadditive quantity. The key step of the proof relies on the observation in [ 11 , Lemma A.7] , which breaks the control of subadditive quantities with mixing condition into its mean and the random fluctuation. It also applies to the mapping \Lambda\mapsto J(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p,q) in our setting: for every p\in B_{1} , there exists a constant C independent of \rho such that (8.6) \displaystyle J(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)\leqslant 2\mathbf{E}[J(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)]+\mathcal{O}_{1}(C\lambda 3^{-(m-n)d}). Here we have the freedom to choose the parameter n\in(0,m)\cap\mathbb{N}_{+} , which will determine t in ( 8.4 ) by setting t:=d\left(1-\frac{n}{m}\right)\in(0,d) . Therefore, it suffices to study of the decay of the mean part \mathbf{E}[J(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)] and the proof is quite similar to what we have done in Section 5 , where the main steps are Lemmas 5.2 and 5.4 , and Proposition 5.3 . They can be carried to the disordered setting for the following reasons. (1) Lemma 5.2 depends on the modified Caccioppoli inequality ( 2.10 ), which is valid for each \omega\in\Omega because \{c_{b}^{\omega}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}} satisfies the uniform ellipticity by (1) of Hypothesis 8.1 , and the underlying invariant measure is still Bernoulli measure by (2) of Hypothesis 8.1 . (2) The variance decay in Lemma 5.4 uses the spatial independence, which is ensured by (3) and (4) of Hypothesis 8.1 . More precisely, let v(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},p,q) be the optimizer of J(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},p,q) like ( 4.16 ), we aim to estimate \displaystyle\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\nabla_{x}\left(v(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},p,q)-\ell_{D_{n}^{-1}q-p}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right] \displaystyle\leqslant C3^{-\beta n}+C\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}3^{-\beta(n-k)}\tau_{k}, where the quantity D_{n}(\rho) is defined as \displaystyle D_{n}(\rho):=\mathbf{E}[{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n})^{-1}]^{-1}, and \tau_{n} is defined as \tau_{n}=\sup_{p,q\in B_{1}}\mathbf{E}[J(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},p,q)-J(\cdot,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n+1},p,q)]. We write v_{n} as a shorthand for v(\omega,\rho,{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n},p,q) and v_{n-1,z} as a shorthand of v(\omega,\rho,z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n-1},p,q) , then we have the decomposition \displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}}\nabla_{x}\left(v_{n}-\ell_{D_{n}^{-1}q-p}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,n-1}}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n-1}}\nabla_{x}\left(v_{n-1,z}-\ell_{D_{n-1}^{-1}q-p}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,n-1}}\sum_{x\in z+{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{n-1}}\nabla_{x}\left(v_{n-1,z}-v_{n}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}} \displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{\lambda}|D_{n}^{-1}q-D_{n-1}^{-1}q|. The key is the cancellation in the first term under \mathbf{E}[\left\langle{\cdots}\right\rangle_{\rho}] . After expanding the sum, we not only use the finite-range dependence of \eta\mapsto c^{\omega}_{b}(\eta) to obtain the independence over \mathbb{P}_{\rho} , but we also use the unit-range dependence of \mathbf{P} . (3) The L^{2} -flatness estimate in Proposition 5.3 should also be carried under the expectation \mathbf{E} . It relies on the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality ( 3.19 ), which does not involve the jump rate. Then we further develop it, and the variance decay in Lemma 5.4 applies to the typical case ( 5.15 ), which has been discussed as above. For the atypical case ( 5.16 ), the L^{\infty} estimate also applies since it only requires the log-Sobolev inequality (see the proof in Appendix B ), which remains valid thanks to the uniform ellipticity of the jump rate by (1) of Hypothesis 8.1 . ∎ One can deduce further results built on Theorem 8.2 . Appendix A Sub-additivity and Whitney inequality A lot of results on quantitative homogenization are stated for the triadic cubes, but they actually hold for the general domain with reasonable boundary regularity, and such generalization only relies on the sub-additivity and a nice decomposition. We state this observation under {\mathbb{R}^{d}} setting, and one can adapt it easily in lattice models. In the following statement, a triadic cube Q in {\mathbb{R}^{d}} is an open set of type z+(-\frac{3^{m}}{2},\frac{3^{m}}{2})^{d} , where z\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}} is called its center and 3^{m} with m\in\mathbb{Z} is called its size and is denoted by \size(Q) . Especially, we denote by {\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m}=(-\frac{3^{m}}{2},\frac{3^{m}}{2})^{d} as the cube centered at 0 and of size 3^{m} in this section. We also denote by |U| the {\mathbb{R}^{d}} -Lebesgue measure for Borel set U , and by \sigma(\mathcal{C}) the area of the (d-1) -dimensional surface \mathcal{C} . Lemma A.1 . Let the quantity \nu be defined on the bounded open sets of {\mathbb{R}^{d}} with Lipschitz boundary and satisfy the following properties. (1) (Spatial homogeneous) For any z\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}} and open set U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}} , \nu(z+U)=\nu(U) . (2) (Sub-additivity) Given disjoint open sets \{U_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n} such that they partition U in the sense that U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}\subseteq U and |U\setminus(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i})|=0 , then we have (A.1) \displaystyle\nu(U)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{|U_{i}|}{|U|}\nu(U_{i}). (3) (Decay for triadic cubes) There exist two finite positive constants C,\alpha , such that for any triadic cube {\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m} , we have (A.2) \displaystyle\nu({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{m})\leqslant C(3^{-\alpha m}\wedge 1). Then there exists a positive constant C^{\prime} , such that for any open set U circumscribed by piecewise C^{1} surfaces, we have (A.3) \displaystyle\nu(U)\leqslant C^{\prime}\left(\frac{\sigma(\partial U)\diam(U)^{(1-\alpha)\vee 0}}{|U|}\wedge 1\right). Remark A.2 . As an application, for the cube \Lambda_{L}=(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{d} with L\geqslant 1 , we have \nu(\Lambda_{L})\leqslant C^{\prime}L^{-(\alpha\wedge 1)} and it gives a polynomial decay in function of the diameter. Proof. The case for \diam(U)\leqslant 1 is trivial and we focus on the case that U of large diameter. The proof relies on the standard Whitney decomposition, which will give a family of open triadic cubes \{Q_{j}\}_{j\geqslant 0} of nice properties; see [ 46 , Appendix J] for its construction and proof. In our proof, the useful properties from such decomposition are • \{Q_{j}\}_{j\geqslant 0} are disjoint and they partition U in the sense of (2) in Lemma A.1 ; • \sqrt{d}\size(Q_{j})\leqslant\dist(Q_{j},\partial U)\leqslant 4\sqrt{d}\size(Q_{j}) . Applying the sub-additivity to \{Q_{j}\}_{j\geqslant 0} and by passing to the limit, we have (A.4) \displaystyle\nu(U)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{|Q_{i}|}{|U|}\nu(Q_{i}). Let m be the positive integer such that (A.5) \displaystyle 3^{m-1}\leqslant\diam(U)\leqslant 3^{m}, then we classify the cubes by their sizes \displaystyle I_{k}:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}:\size(Q_{i})=3^{k}\}. Using the second properties listed above about the decomposition, all the cubes in I_{k} stay in distance 4\sqrt{d}3^{k} from the boundary, then we have (A.6) \displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I_{k}}Q_{i}\subseteq\{x\in U:\dist(x,\partial U)\leqslant 5\sqrt{d}3^{k}\}. Since U admits piece-wise C^{1} boundary, we denote by \partial U=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{j} , where different pieces \mathcal{C}_{j} only have intersection of null set. Then we have the following volume estimate (A.7) \sum_{i\in I_{k}}|Q_{i}|\leqslant\left|\{x\in U:\dist(x,\partial U)\leqslant 5\sqrt{d}3^{k}\}\right|\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left|\{x\in U:\dist(x,\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})\leqslant 5\sqrt{d}3^{k}\}\right|\\ \leqslant 10\sqrt{d}3^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{j})=10\sqrt{d}3^{k}\sigma(\partial U). Then we put these estimates back to the cubes from Whitney decomposition ( A.4 ) to obtain \displaystyle\nu(U) \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{|U|}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{k}}|Q_{i}|\right)\nu({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{k})+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{1}{|U|}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{k}}|Q_{i}|\right)\nu({\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{k}) \displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C\sigma(\partial U)}{|U|}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{0}3^{k}+\frac{C\sigma(\partial U)}{|U|}\sum_{k=1}^{m}3^{(1-\alpha)k} \displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C\sigma(\partial U)}{|U|}3^{m(1-\alpha)\vee 0}. Here apply ( A.2 ) and ( A.7 ) from the first line to the second line, then we use ( A.5 ) to conclude the proof. ∎ Appendix B Proof of L^{\infty} norm using mixing time In this part, we prove Lemma 4.14 . Such estimate should be generally valid for Markov chain, and we state the case of Kawasaki dynamics. Lemma B.1 . There exists a constant C(\lambda,d)<\infty , such that for any connected domain \Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}} of diameter L and any two functions u,f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} satisfying \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}u=f, then for any N\in\mathbb{N}_{+} we have (B.1) \displaystyle\left\|{u-\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant CL^{2}\log L\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}. Proof. Let P_{t}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{+} be the transition probability of the continuous-time Kawasaki dynamics generated by \mathcal{L}_{\Lambda} with reflect boundary condition, which has \mu_{\Lambda,N}=\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N} as its stationary measure for any N\in\mathbb{N}_{+} . Then we use the Duhamel’s formula (B.2) \displaystyle u(\eta)-\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}=\int_{0}^{\infty}(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\,{\mathrm{d}}t, which is well-defined thanks to the spectral gap and exponential decay of the mapping t\mapsto\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\Lambda,N}[P_{t}f] . Then we estimate the L^{\infty} norm. We notice that \displaystyle\left\|{(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty} \displaystyle=\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}}\left(\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}P_{t}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})f(\eta^{\prime})-\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\mu_{\Lambda,N}(\eta^{\prime})f(\eta^{\prime})\right) (B.3) \displaystyle\leqslant 2\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}}\left\|{P_{t}(\eta,\cdot)-\mu_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{TV}\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}. Because we assume reflect boundary condition for the Kawasaki dynamics associated to \mathcal{L}_{\Lambda} , the term P_{t}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}) is non-zero only when \displaystyle\forall x\in\Lambda^{c},\qquad\eta_{x}=\eta^{\prime}_{x}. Thus, the sum \sum_{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}} is actually over a finite set and well-defined. Then we denote by \left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{TV} the total variation distance and make use of its definition [ 60 , (4.7)] . By convention in [ 60 , (4.22),(4.30),(4.31)] , we also define \displaystyle d(t) \displaystyle:=\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}}\left\|{P_{t}(\eta,\cdot)-\mu_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{TV}, \displaystyle t_{\operatorname{mix}} \displaystyle:=\inf\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:d(t)<\frac{1}{4}\right\}. Then t\mapsto d(t) is decreasing and satisfies (see [ 60 , (4.33)] ) \displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad d(nt_{\operatorname{mix}})\leqslant 2^{-n}. We put this observation and ( B.3 ) back to ( B.2 ) \displaystyle\left\|{(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty} \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{nt_{\operatorname{mix}}}^{(n+1)t_{\operatorname{mix}}}\left\|{(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty}\,{\mathrm{d}}t \displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2d(nt_{\operatorname{mix}})\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}t_{\operatorname{mix}} \displaystyle\leqslant 2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{-n}\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}t_{\operatorname{mix}} \displaystyle=2\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}t_{\operatorname{mix}}. One classical method to obtain the mixing time of Markov process is the log-Sobolev inequality; see [ 26 , (1.8)] . In our case, we use the log-Sobolev inequality on general Kawasaki dynamics developed in [ 62 , Theorem 3] ; see also [ 65 , (2)] and discussion there. This gives us (B.4) \displaystyle t_{\operatorname{mix}}\leqslant C(d,\lambda)L^{2}\log\log{L^{d}\choose N}\leqslant\widetilde{C}(d,\lambda)L^{2}\log L, which yields ( B.1 ). ∎ Remark B.2 . The steps before ( B.4 ) is standard for all the reversible Markov processes. We apply the log-Sobolev inequality for the mixing time, because the jump rate in this paper depends on the local configuration. The spectral gap inequality [ 62 , Theorem 2] is also enough to conclude ( B.4 ) with a slightly larger bound L^{d+2}\log L . We remark some other recent progresses [ 72 , 56 , 65 ] on the mixing time of the exclusion processes (i.e. the constant-speed Kawasaki dynamics). Their generalization on the non-gradient models is an interesting but challenging question. Proof of Lemma 4.4 . We take u(\Lambda,q) for example, which is the solution of \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}u(\Lambda,q)=\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda_{L}^{*}}}\pi_{b}\ell_{q}. Therefore, we apply ( B.1 ) with \left\|{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda_{L}^{*}}}\pi_{b}\ell_{q}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant C(\lambda,d)L^{d} . The case for v(\rho,\Lambda,\xi) can be done similarly. ∎ Remark B.3 . For 2\leqslant p<\infty , we have \|u-\langle u\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\|_{p}\leqslant CL^{2}\|f\|_{p} without \log L . Indeed, we may apply Riesz–Thorin interpolation theorem for two inequalities \|P_{t}f-\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2\|f\|_{\infty} and \|P_{t}f-\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\|_{2}\leqslant e^{-cL^{-2}t}\|f\|_{2} for some c>0 , which follows from the spectral gap estimate for \mathcal{L}_{\Lambda} . Appendix C Proofs of lemmas and proposition in Section 7.3 Here we complete the proofs of Lemma 7.6 (calculation of \partial_{t}h_{N}(t) ), Proposition 7.8 (refined one-block estimate), Lemma 7.11 (a key lemma) and Lemma 7.12 (rewriting O(N) -looking term to O(1) ) postponed from Sections 7.3.1 , 7.3.2 and 7.3.3 . These are gathered here, since they are shown based on known results with some modification providing fine error estimates. C.1. Proof of Lemma 7.6 (calculation of \partial_{t}h_{N}(t) ) Throughout this proof, we use the following shorthand notations for simplicity \displaystyle F\equiv F_{N},\qquad\psi_{t}\equiv\psi_{t}^{N},\qquad f_{t}\equiv f_{t}^{N},\qquad Z_{t}\equiv Z_{\lambda(t,\cdot),F_{N}}. Recall that h_{N}(t) is the relative entropy per volume defined by ( 7.6 ) with respect to the local equilibrium \psi_{t}=\psi_{\lambda(t,\cdot),F}^{N} in ( 7.5 ). Then, we apply the estimate (C.1) \partial_{t}h_{N}(t)\leqslant N^{-d}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{N}}\psi_{t}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{N}^{*}\psi_{t}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}\right)\cdot f_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N}, which holds for a large class of Markovian models, where \mathcal{L}_{N}^{*} denotes the dual operator of the generator \mathcal{L}_{N}(=N^{2}\mathcal{L}) with respect to the measure \nu^{N} ; see [ 80 ] Lemma 1. The proof is divided into four steps. Step 1. (First term in ( C.1 )) The integrand on the right-hand side of ( C.1 ) was already computed in the proof of Lemma 3.1 of [ 36 ] , but only with the error estimate o(1) . Let us record the computation (3.2) in [ 36 ] here to make the error term clear. Noting the symmetry of the Kawasaki generator \mathcal{L} : \mathcal{L}^{*}=\mathcal{L} , (C.2) \displaystyle\qquad N^{-d}\psi_{t}^{-1}N^{2}\mathcal{L}\psi_{t} \displaystyle= \displaystyle\frac{N^{2-d}}{2}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\bigg[\exp\Big\{(\lambda(t,x/N)-\lambda(t,y/N))(\eta_{y}-\eta_{x})\phantom{\sum_{z\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}} \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{N}\pi_{x,y}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\left(\nabla\lambda(t,z/N)\cdot\tau_{z}F\right)\Big\}-1\bigg] \displaystyle= \displaystyle-\frac{N^{1-d}}{2}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\Omega_{x,y}+\frac{N^{-d}}{4}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\Omega_{x,y}^{2} \displaystyle-\frac{N^{-d}}{4}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda(t,x/N)(y_{i}-x_{i})(y_{j}-x_{j})(\eta_{y}-\eta_{x}) \displaystyle+\frac{N^{-d}}{2}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda(t,x/N)\pi_{x,y}\left(\sum_{z\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}(z_{j}-x_{j})\tau_{z}F_{i}\right)+Q_{1,N}, where the error term Q_{1,N}\equiv Q_{1,N}(\lambda,F) is defined as the difference between the expressions in the middle two lines and the last three lines in ( C.2 ). Recall ( 7.28 ) for \Omega_{x,y} . This error is estimated as (C.3) \displaystyle|Q_{1,N}|\leqslant \displaystyle CN^{-1}\Big(1+\|\nabla\lambda\|_{3,\infty}\Big)^{3}\Big(1+r(F)^{d+2}\|F\|_{\infty}\Big)^{3}, if the condition ( 7.30 ) and accordingly |\varepsilon_{N}|\leqslant C below are satisfied for some C>0 . Here, we use \varepsilon_{N}\equiv\varepsilon_{N,x,y} to denote the term given in the braces in ( C.2 ). Indeed, to show ( C.3 ), we expand the second to the third lines of ( C.2 ) as \exp\{\varepsilon_{N}\}-1=\varepsilon_{N}+\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{N}^{3}),\quad|\varepsilon_{N}|\leqslant C. Then, by Taylor’s formula, we have \displaystyle\lambda(t,x/N)-\lambda(t,y/N)=- \displaystyle\tfrac{1}{N}\nabla\lambda(t,x/N)\cdot(y-x) \displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2N^{2}}(y-x)\cdot\nabla^{2}\lambda(t,x/N)(y-x)+O\Big(N^{-3}{\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty}}\Big), and therefore \varepsilon_{N}=-\tfrac{1}{N}\Omega_{x,y}+\tfrac{1}{N}\overline{\Omega}_{x,y}-\tfrac{1}{2N^{2}}(y-x)\cdot\nabla^{2}\lambda(t,x/N)(y-x)(\eta_{y}-\eta_{x})+O\Big(N^{-3}{\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty}}\Big), where \displaystyle\overline{\Omega}_{x,y}\equiv\overline{\Omega}_{x,y}(\eta)=\pi_{x,y}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\left(\nabla\lambda(t,z/N)-\nabla\lambda(t,x/N)\right)\cdot\tau_{z}F) \displaystyle=N^{-1}\left(\nabla^{2}\lambda(t,x/N)\pi_{x,y}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}(z-x)\right)\cdot\tau_{z}F+O\left(N^{-2}\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty}\left|\pi_{x,y}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}(z-x)^{\otimes 2}\tau_{z}F\right|\right), where x^{\otimes 2}=(x_{i}x_{j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d} is a d\times d matrix. Thus, Q_{1,N}\equiv Q_{1,N}(\lambda,F) is given by \displaystyle Q_{1,N}= \displaystyle\frac{N^{2-d}}{2}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}O\Big(N^{-3}\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty}(1+r(F)^{d+2}\|F\|_{\infty})\Big) \displaystyle+\frac{N^{2-d}}{4}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\big(\varepsilon_{N}^{2}-N^{-2}\Omega_{x,y}^{2}\big)+O\left(N^{2-d}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\varepsilon_{N}^{3}\right). The first term (its absolute value) is bounded by CN^{-1}\|\nabla^{3}\lambda\|_{\infty}(1+r(F)^{d+2}\|F\|_{\infty}). By noting that |\Omega_{x,y}|\leqslant\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}(1+r(F)^{d}\|F\|_{\infty}) , the second is bounded by \displaystyle\left|\frac{N^{2-d}}{4}\sum_{x\sim y}c_{x,y}\big(\varepsilon_{N}+\tfrac{1}{N}\Omega_{x,y}\big)\big(\varepsilon_{N}-\tfrac{1}{N}\Omega_{x,y}\big)\right| \displaystyle\leqslant CN^{-1}\Big(1+\|\nabla\lambda\|_{3,\infty}\Big)^{2}\Big(1+r(F)^{d+2}\|F\|_{\infty}\Big)^{2}, see the same estimate given in Section 9.1 of [ 34 ] , if necessary. Similarly, the third is bounded by \displaystyle CN^{2}\sup_{x,y,t}|\varepsilon_{N}^{3}|\leqslant CN^{-1}\Big(1+\|\nabla\lambda\|_{3,\infty}\Big)^{3}\Big(1+r(F)^{d+2}\|F\|_{\infty}\Big)^{3}, also see Section 9.1 of [ 34 ] , if necessary. Summarizing these estimates for three terms of Q_{1,N}(\lambda,F) , we obtain ( C.3 ) for Q_{1,N}(\lambda,F) . Step 2. (Second term in ( C.1 )) Recalling ( 7.5 ) for \psi_{t}\,(=\psi_{t}^{N}) , the second term in the integrand on the right-hand side of ( C.1 ) is rewritten as (C.4) \displaystyle N^{-d}\psi_{t}^{-1}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}= \displaystyle-N^{-d}Z_{t}^{-1}\partial_{t}Z_{t} \displaystyle+N^{-d}\partial_{t}\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\lambda(t,x/N)\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\lambda(t,x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}F\right\}. From ( C.2 ) and ( C.4 ), it follows that (C.5) N^{-d}\psi_{t}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{N}^{*}\psi_{t}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}\right)=\Omega_{1}(\eta)+\Omega_{2}(\eta)+a(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{i,N}, where Q_{1,N} is given in Step 1, \displaystyle Q_{2,N}\equiv Q_{2,N}(\lambda,F)=-N^{-d-1}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\nabla\dot{\lambda}(t,x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}F, and (C.6) a(t):=N^{-d}Z_{t}^{-1}\partial_{t}Z_{t}=E^{\psi_{t}}\left[N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\dot{\lambda}(t,x/N)\eta_{x}\right]+O\Big(N^{-1}\|\nabla\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}\Big). Recall ( 7.26 ) for \Omega_{1} . The last equality for a(t) is seen from ( C.4 ) by noting that E^{\psi_{t}}\left[N^{-d}\psi_{t}^{-1}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}\right]=0 and (C.7) |Q_{2,N}|\leqslant N^{-1}\|\nabla\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}. Thus, a(t)=N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\dot{\lambda}(t,x/N)\rho(t,x/N)+Q_{3,N}+O\Big(N^{-1}\|\nabla\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}\Big), where the error Q_{3,N}\equiv Q_{3,N}(\lambda,F) is given by Q_{3,N}=N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\dot{\lambda}(t,x/N)\Big(E^{\psi_{t}}\left[\eta_{x}\right]-\rho(t,x/N)\Big). We only need to estimate |E^{\psi_{t}}\left[\eta_{x}\right]-\rho(t,x/N)| . Step 3. (Proof of the error estimate ( 7.29 )) To give an estimate for Q_{3,N}\equiv Q_{3,N}(\lambda,F) , we note the r -Markov property of \psi_{t} (or P^{\psi_{t}} ) with r=r(F) (cf. Lemma A.1 of [ 34 ] ) to see (C.8) E^{\psi_{t}}[\eta_{x}]=E^{\psi_{t}}\left[E^{P_{\{x\}}^{\omega,F}}[\eta_{x}]\right], where P_{\{x\}}^{\omega,F}(\eta_{x})=P^{\psi_{t}}(\{\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{N};\zeta_{x}=\eta_{x}\}|\mathcal{F}_{\{x\}^{c}})(\omega) for \eta_{x}\in\{0,1\} and \omega\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}\setminus\{x\}} , \overline{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{r(F),x} . Then, (C.9) \displaystyle E^{P_{\{x\}}^{\omega,F}}[\eta_{x}] \displaystyle=\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}^{-1}A_{\omega,F}, where \displaystyle A_{\omega,F} \displaystyle:=\sum_{\eta_{x}\in\{0,1\}}\eta_{x}e^{\lambda(t,x/N)\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}:\,\text{supp}\,\tau_{y}F\subseteq\overline{\Lambda}}(\nabla\lambda(t,y/N)\cdot\tau_{y}F(\eta_{x}\cdot\omega))}, \displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F} \displaystyle:=\sum_{\eta_{x}\in\{0,1\}}e^{\lambda(t,x/N)\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}:\,\text{supp}\,\tau_{y}F\subseteq\overline{\Lambda}}(\nabla\lambda(t,y/N)\cdot\tau_{y}F(\eta_{x}\cdot\omega))}. Noting that E^{P_{\{x\}}^{\omega,0}}[\eta_{x}]=\rho(t,x/N) taking F\equiv 0 , we have E^{P_{\{x\}}^{\omega,F}}[\eta_{x}]-\rho(t,x/N)=\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}^{-1}A_{\omega,F}-\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,0}^{-1}A_{\omega,0}. A simple estimate shows \displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{0,\omega}e^{-\frac{c}{N}r(F)^{d}\|\nabla\lambda(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}}\leqslant\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}\leqslant\widetilde{Z}_{0,\omega}e^{\frac{c}{N}r(F)^{d}\|\nabla\lambda(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}}, \displaystyle A_{0,\omega}e^{-\frac{c}{N}r(F)^{d}\|\nabla\lambda(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}}\leqslant A_{\omega,F}\leqslant A_{0,\omega}e^{\frac{c}{N}r(F)^{d}\|\nabla\lambda(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}}, for some c>0 . Therefore, taking the expectation in \omega under P^{\psi_{t}} , we obtain by ( C.8 ) \displaystyle|E^{\psi_{t}}\left[\eta_{x}\right]-\rho(t,x/N)| \displaystyle=\left|E^{\psi_{t}}\left[\frac{A_{0,\omega}}{\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}}\Big(\frac{A_{\omega,F}}{A_{0,\omega}}-\frac{\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}}{\widetilde{Z}_{0,\omega}}\Big)\right]\right| \displaystyle\leqslant C_{1}\Big|e^{\frac{c}{N}r(F)^{d}\|\nabla\lambda(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}}-1\bigg| \displaystyle\leqslant C_{2}N^{-1}r(F)^{d}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}, if F satisfies the condition ( 7.30 ), i.e., N^{-1}r(F)^{d}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1 ; note that 0\leqslant A_{0,\omega}/\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}=A_{0,\omega}/\widetilde{Z}_{0,\omega}\cdot\widetilde{Z}_{0,\omega}/\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}\leqslant e^{c} under this condition. This leads to the bound on Q_{3,N}\equiv Q_{3,N}(\lambda,F) : (C.10) \displaystyle|Q_{3,N}|\leqslant CN^{-1}\|\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty}\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}r(F)^{d}\|F\|_{\infty}. By ( C.3 ), ( C.7 ) and ( C.10 ), we obtain ( 7.29 ), and this concludes the proof of Lemma 7.6 . C.2. Proof of Proposition 7.8 (refined one-block estimate) We follow the argument in Section 3 of [ 37 ] , where the Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics \eta^{N}(t) was considered. Note that although the gradient condition was assumed in [ 37 ] , it was not used in Section 3. Recalling \hat{\Omega}_{x} in ( 7.32 ), we decompose as (C.11) \displaystyle E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}\hat{\Omega}_{x}{\mathrm{d}}s\bigg|\bigg] \displaystyle\leqslant \displaystyle E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}m_{x}{\mathrm{d}}s\bigg|\bigg]+E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}E^{\nu_{1/2}}[\hat{\Omega}_{x}\mid\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L}]{\mathrm{d}}s\bigg|\bigg], where \nu_{1/2} is the Bernoulli measure with mean \rho=1/2 on \mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}} or on \mathcal{X}_{N}=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}} and \displaystyle m_{x}\equiv m_{x}(\eta):=\hat{\Omega}_{x}-E^{\nu_{1/2}}[\hat{\Omega}_{x}\mid\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L}]. Precisely saying, the three expectations in ( C.11 ) are taken for the process \eta^{N}(\cdot) , so that \hat{\Omega}_{x} in the left-hand side and m_{x} in the right-hand side are understood as \hat{\Omega}_{x}(\eta^{N}(s)) and m_{x}(\eta^{N}(s)) , respectively, and also we replace \eta to \eta^{N}(s) in the conditional expectation, which is a function of \eta , in the last expectation. Lemma 3.1 of [ 37 ] shown for the Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics applies for the first term in the right-hand side of ( C.11 ). Recall we assume ( 7.33 ) for a_{t,x} : |a_{t,x}|\leqslant M , also ( 7.34 ) for L . Lemma C.1 . (cf. Lemma 3.1 of [ 37 ] , Lemma 9.1 of [ 34 ] ) Let \gamma=\gamma(N)>0 be given and assume \gamma ML^{d+2}\leqslant\delta N^{2} for some \delta>0 small enough. Then, for every t\in[0,T] and N large enough (C.12) E\left[\left|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}m_{x}{\mathrm{d}}s\right|\right]\leqslant C\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma L^{d+2}}{N^{2}}M^{2}\|\Omega\|_{\infty}^{2}\right), for some C=C_{T}>0 . In particular, choosing \gamma=NL^{-(d+2)/2}/M(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1) , the right-hand side is bounded by 2CN^{-1}L^{(d+2)/2}M(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1). For this choice of \gamma , the above condition for \gamma is satisfied if the condition ( 7.34 ) stated in Proposition 7.8 holds. Proof. We may adjust the proof of Lemma 3.1 of [ 37 ] in our setting. The differences are two-fold. The generator in [ 37 ] was N^{2}\mathcal{L}+KL_{G} with Glauber generator L_{G} with time change factor K\geqslant 1 . In our case, K=0 . The second difference is the assumption on a_{t,x} . We assume ( 7.33 ): |a_{t,x}|\leqslant M , while it was |a_{t,x}|\leqslant CK^{\theta}/[t(T-t)]^{\Theta} for some \theta\geqslant 0,\Theta\in[0,1) depending on the Glauber speed K and also t\in[0,T] . Our assumption ( 7.33 ) is simpler. We only point out the necessary changes in the proof. First by applying the entropy inequality with respect to the Bernoulli measure \nu^{N}=\nu_{1/2}^{N} on \mathcal{X}_{N} , as in (3.3) of [ 37 ] , we obtain (C.13) \displaystyle E\left[\left|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}m_{x}{\mathrm{d}}s\right|\right] \displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\frac{\log 2}{\gamma}+\frac{N^{-d}}{\gamma}\log E^{\nu^{N}}\left[\exp\left|\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}m_{x}{\mathrm{d}}s\right|\right]. To estimate the second term on the right-hand side of ( C.13 ), instead of the assumption (1.19) in [ 37 ] for a_{t,x} (i.e. |a_{t,x}|\leqslant CK^{\theta}/[t(T-t)]^{\Theta} , \theta\geqslant 0,\Theta\in[0,1) ), we have ( 7.33 ): |a_{t,x}|\leqslant M for all t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d} . In other words, we may take \Theta=0 and M for CK^{\theta} in (1.19) of [ 37 ] . Furthermore, letting \varepsilon=\varepsilon(N)\downarrow 0 , the first term in the concluding estimate in Lemma 3.1 of [ 37 ] becomes negligible, since \varepsilon^{1-\Theta}K^{\theta}=\varepsilon M/C\to 0 . Then, since there is no Glauber part (i.e. K=0 ), we do not have the term K\langle-L_{G}\psi,\psi\rangle_{\nu_{\beta}} in (3.7) (recall \nu_{\beta}=\nu^{N} ) so that no C^{\prime}KN^{d} in (3.8) in the proof of Lemma 3.1 of [ 37 ] . This K gave the second term \frac{K}{\gamma} in the concluding estimate in Lemma 3.1. Instead, we only have \frac{\log 2}{\gamma} from ( C.13 ) and this gives the first term \frac{C}{\gamma} in our estimate ( C.12 ). For the third term in the concluding estimate in Lemma 3.1 ( \varepsilon^{2\Theta}=\varepsilon^{0}=1 ), at the last part of its proof, we had \langle(-\mathcal{L}_{L,x})^{-1}(a_{t,x}m_{x})a_{t,x}m_{x}\rangle_{x+\Lambda_{L},j}\leqslant\frac{1}{\operatorname{gap}(j,L)}\sup_{t,x,\eta}|a_{t,x}m_{x}|^{2}, where \operatorname{gap}(j,L)\geqslant C/L^{2} as indicated above this estimate in [ 37 ] . Thus, under our assumption ( 7.33 ) on a_{t,x} , estimating as \sup_{t,x,\eta}|a_{t,x}m_{x}|^{2}\leqslant(2M)^{2}\|\Omega\|_{\infty}^{2}, we obtain the estimate ( C.12 ). Note that the condition ‘ \gamma ML^{d+2}\leqslant\delta N^{2} for some \delta>0 small enough’ was used for the denominator in (3.10) of [ 37 ] (with M instead of CK^{\theta} and \varepsilon^{-\Theta}=1 ), derived by Rayleigh estimate, to stay uniformly positive, say \geqslant 1/2 . This determines how small \delta>0 needs to be. This is the condition (3.2) in [ 37 ] with \varepsilon^{-\Theta}=\varepsilon^{0}=1 and M/C instead of K^{\theta} . The second assertion with the special choice of \gamma is straightforward. ∎ Let us continue the proof of Proposition 7.8 . For the second term in ( C.11 ), we apply Theorem 4.1 of [ 19 ] (taking \Lambda=\Lambda_{L} ) which provides the equivalence of ensembles for general Gibbs measures with precise convergence rate: For any \varepsilon\in(0,1/4) , there exists C=C_{\varepsilon}>0 such that for all N sufficiently large (C.14) \sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\max_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}}\left|E^{\nu_{\rho}}[\tau_{x}\Omega\mid\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L}]-\langle\Omega\rangle(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})\right|\leqslant\frac{C}{L^{d}}|\supp\Omega|\ \|\Omega\|_{\infty}. Combining the two estimates given in Lemma C.1 and ( C.14 ) with \rho=1/2 and |\supp\Omega|\leqslant r(\Omega)^{d}\leqslant L^{d(1-4\varepsilon)} and writing the exponent as a=1-4\varepsilon\in(0,1) , the proof of Proposition 7.8 is concluded. C.3. Proof of Lemma 7.11 (a key lemma) Recall that J(t,v)\in C^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{n}) , G(\eta)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{n} and {\mathbf{U}}(\rho)\in C([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n}) are given. For a box \Lambda_{L} , we decompose \eta as \xi=\eta|_{\Lambda_{L}} and \zeta=\eta|_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}} . We write G as G(\eta)=G_{\zeta}(\xi) regarding it as a function of \xi . We assume \langle G_{\zeta}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}=0 for every \zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}} and M:0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}| . The proof is divided into three steps. It is given by combining that of Lemma 6.1 of [ 36 ] and the calculation developed in that of Lemma 3.1 of [ 37 ] for Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics. Step 1. (Application of entropy inequality and Feynman-Kac formula) Setting W_{N,t}(\eta)=N\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}J(t,x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}G-\beta\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}J(t,x/N)\cdot{\mathbf{U}}(\overline{\eta}_{x}^{L})J(t,x/N), by the entropy inequality noting that h_{N}(P^{f_{0}}|P^{\nu^{N}})\leqslant C (recall the normalized entropy defined by ( 7.4 )) ( P^{f_{0}} denotes the distribution on the path space D([0,T],\mathcal{X}_{N}) of \eta^{N}(\cdot) with the initial distribution f_{0}^{N}\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N} ) at the process level, we have \displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}E^{f_{s}^{N}}[N^{-d}W_{N,s}(\eta)]{\mathrm{d}}s \displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\beta}N^{-d}\log{E^{\nu^{N}}\left[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\beta W_{N,s}(\eta^{N}(s)){\mathrm{d}}s}\right]}+\frac{C}{\beta}, for every \beta>0 ; recall \nu^{N}=\nu_{1/2}^{N} and E^{\nu^{N}}[\,\cdot\,] means the expectation under the process \eta^{N}(\cdot) with the initial distribution \nu^{N} . Then, by Feynman–Kac formula, similar to (3.6) of [ 37 ] (with \varepsilon=0,\varepsilon^{1-\Theta}=0 in the sense that we explained in the proof of Lemma C.1 in Section C.2 ) shown for the Glauber–Kawasaki dynamics, the right-hand side is bounded by (C.15) \displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}N^{-d}\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{N,\beta}(s){\mathrm{d}}s+\frac{C}{\beta}, where (C.16) \Omega_{N,\beta}(t)=\sup_{\psi:\int\psi^{2}d\nu^{N}=1}\Big\{E^{\nu^{N}}[\beta W_{N,t}\psi^{2}]-N^{2}E^{\nu^{N}}[\psi(-\mathcal{L}\psi)]\Big\}. Recall that the generator of the process \eta^{N}(\cdot) is \mathcal{L}_{N}=N^{2}\mathcal{L} . Step 2. (Error estimate for \Omega_{N,\beta}(t) ) Since \Omega_{N,\beta}(t) is the same quantity as (6.3) of [ 36 ] , the same bound as [ 36 ] holds for \Omega_{N,\beta}(t) until the very end of the proof of Lemma 6.1 of [ 36 ] . The only difference is that, in (6.7) of [ 36 ] , the limit was taken as N\to\infty , but here we have to derive an error estimate. Indeed, the first term in ( C.15 ) is bounded by t times the expression on line 4, p.28 of [ 36 ] , i.e. by adjusting the notation, it is bounded by (C.17) t\sup_{|q|\leqslant\|J\|_{\infty}}\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}}}\sup_{M:0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|}\left[\frac{N^{2}}{\beta|(\Lambda_{L})^{*}|}\Omega_{N,\beta,q,G}^{M,L,\zeta}-\beta\,q\cdot{\mathbf{U}}(M/|\Lambda_{L}|)q\right], where \Omega_{N,\beta,q,G}^{M,L,\zeta} is the largest eigenvalue of the symmetric operator \mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta}+V with V=N^{-1}\beta|(\Lambda_{L})^{*}|\,q\cdot G_{\zeta} in L^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L},M},\nu_{\Lambda_{L},M}) ; recall ( 6.77 ) and its above for \mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta} and \nu_{\Lambda_{L},M} . By Theorem 6.1 of [ 36 ] (Rayleigh-Schrödinger bound for general Markov generator), \Omega_{N,\beta,q,G}^{M,L,\zeta} is bounded as (C.18) \Omega_{N,\beta,q,G}^{M,L,\zeta}\leqslant\langle V(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}V\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}+Q_{N,L,\beta}(V), where the upper bound of the error is given by Q_{N,L,\beta}(V):=4\|V\|_{\infty}^{3}\left(\sup_{f:\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}=0}\frac{\langle f^{2}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}}{\langle-f\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta}f\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}}\right)^{2}. Since we have a spectral gap estimate for the Kawasaki generator: (C.19) \displaystyle\langle-f\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta}f\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}\geqslant C_{0}L^{-2}\langle f^{2}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}, for f:\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}=0 , with a constant C_{0}>0 independent of \zeta,M,L (see [ 62 ] or Appendix A of [ 36 ] ), recalling the definition of V , we have a bound for the error term: \displaystyle Q_{N,L,\beta}(V)\leqslant 4N^{-3}\beta^{3}|(\Lambda_{L})^{*}|^{3}\|q\cdot G\|_{\infty}^{3}\,\big(C_{0}^{-1}L^{2}\big)^{2}. Therefore, in ( C.17 ), \displaystyle t\frac{N^{2}}{\beta|(\Lambda_{L})^{*}|}Q_{N,L,\beta}(V)\leqslant C_{T}N^{-1}\beta^{2}L^{(2d+4)}\|G\|_{\infty}^{3}\|J\|_{\infty}^{3}, for q:|q|\leqslant\|J\|_{\infty} . This is the estimate ( 7.42 ) for Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(J,G) in Lemma 7.11 multiplied by \beta^{2} . The rest in ( C.17 ) is, by ( C.18 ), \displaystyle t\sup_{|q|\leqslant\|J\|_{\infty}}\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}}}\sup_{M:0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|}\left[\frac{N^{2}}{\beta|(\Lambda_{L})^{*}|}\langle V(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}V\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}-\beta\,q\cdot{\mathbf{U}}(M/|\Lambda_{L}|)q\right]. This coincides with the variational term in Lemma 7.11 recalling the definition of V and noting that |(\Lambda_{L})^{*}|\leqslant d|\Lambda_{L}| . Thus, we complete the proof of Lemma 7.11 . C.4. Proof of Lemma 7.12 (rewriting O(N) -looking term to O(1) ) We essentially follow the proof of Lemma 3.4 of [ 36 ] , but make it finer. Indeed, it is very similar to the proof of Lemma 4.3 of [ 34 ] (Section 9.4) for the Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics. The difference is only that the term C/\beta in the concluding estimate for Q_{N,L}^{(3)} was CK/\beta for the Glauber-Kawasaki case. This difference is caused when we apply the key lemma, i.e., Lemma 4.2 in [ 34 ] and Lemma 7.11 in our case. Comparing these two lemmas, the difference is only that the error was CK/\beta in Lemma 4.2 in [ 34 ] , while it is C/\beta in our Lemma 7.11 . Therefore, the only difference in the proof is that the estimate (9.25) in [ 34 ] should be replaced by (C.20) \displaystyle\beta T\|\nabla\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}d\,|\Lambda_{L+1}|\sup_{0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L+1}|,\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L+1}^{c}}}\langle G(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L+1},\zeta})^{-1}G\rangle_{\Lambda_{L+1},M} \displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{C}{\beta}+\beta^{2}Q_{N,L+1}^{(1)}(\nabla\lambda,G) in our setting. Otherwise, everything is the same and we may simply copy all the proof of Lemma 4.3 of [ 34 ] as it is. In this way, we can complete the proof of Lemma 7.12 .
